## FX Mid-Year Outlook

## Ten questions (and answers) on currencies in 2H24

Outlook: Dollar to stay elevated on USD’s improving yield advantage and lingering exceptionalism; US elections add upside risks. Yield compression remains a key theme; risk-reward in global carry is unattractive, but G10 carry is more resilient. Funders to underperform tactically, but tariff risk/ lower yields create uncertainty. Housing may provide relief for early-cutter FX. Recession risk is well-priced in loweryielders but not in high-yielders.

Macro Trade Recommendations: Maintain USD length vs EUR, CNH, but tactically as US exceptionalism has moderated but not exhausted. Be surgical with policy r.v. – long NOK vs SEK, EUR and AUD vs NZD. Case against JPY reversion remains compelling; be tactical around intervention risks (take profit AUD/JPY).

Emerging Markets FX: Stay neutral ahead of the US elections amid unresolved macro uncertainties. Favour idiosyncratic carry and FX with attractive valuations. Bullish picks are TRY, INR, MXN, UYU, PHP; Bearish on CZK, CNH, CLP, COP.

FX Derivatives: FX vol unlikely to trend higher despite US elections as firm growth, policy easing and USD length stymies outsized vol pickups. H2 themes: (a) preference for gamma over vol surface; (b) cheap US election hedges; (c) fading European risk premium; (d) RV within the Yen vol complex; (e) defensive long-end vol spreads. Increase elections exposure via 9m USD/CNH digi RR.

FX Technicals: EUR/USD to break below 1.04 in 2H24. USD/JPY looks like a mature bull cycle, but remains intact; watch for a trend reversal in the second half. AUD/USD to pressure broader range support in the months ahead.

## FX Mid-Year Outlook

## Ten questions (and answers) on currencies in 2H24

Dollar to stay elevated due to USD’s improving yield advantage and lingering exceptionalism. Trading approach remains tactical.

Modest Fed easing isn’t sufficient for outsized USD weakness; a growth pick up ex-US is required as well. Stay vigilant for such outcomes given rich USD valuations. Valley of USD smile exists but will be narrow.

US elections add upside risks to USD and are already impacting markets. Transmission to FX could be via: tariff risk (USD positive), Russia policy (EUR-negative), fiscal (USD positive), dollar policy (could be longer term USD-bearish). The first two could be implemented in a timelier manner.

• Election opportunities: Event pricing is tracking prior cycles with USD TWI, CNH vols close to prior highs, MXN middling, but European FX visibly lagging. Medium-term USD/CNH digital risk reversals are attractive.

Rotation away from global carry remains a key theme but will be a process and will require stable/ lower US yields. Some high-yielders could recover but risk-reward in global FX carry is unattractive given yield compression and impending US elections….

...by contrast, G10 carry should be more resilient so long as inflation remains sticky and Fed is high-for-long.

Funders to underperform tactically, but tariff risk/ lower yields create uncertainty; performance to be differentiated. JPY weakness to persist on worst real yields globally/ glacial BoJ. CHF to be relatively resilient on yield/ BoP support.

Central bank policy will impact FX given varying start / size of easing. G10 policy RV involves long USD, AUD, NOK, GBP vs. EUR, NZD, CAD. Watch EUR where sticky inflation could limit easing.

Outlook for EUR/USD remains bearish with target of 1.05. Range of 1.05-1.10 to hold but implications from US elections bias risks to the downside.

Geopolitical risks facing CNY will come to the fore in H2. Paucity of risk premium to keep CNY depreciation intact; risk bias to 7.30 target is squarely to the upside.

FX vol unlikely to trend cleanly higher in H2, despite inevitable US election noise. While VXY is moderately cheap, firm growth, central bank easing, and pre-existing USD length should stymie any outsized vol pickup.

• Housing may provide some relief for early-cutter FX. SEK is our preferred recovery candidate on this basis.

• The largest external deficits are in healthy ranges and thus do not present systemic risks. NZD yield is low for the highest deficit globally. Basic balance for JPY is nearnegative with real yields most negative globally.

● Aside from our baseline recommendations, we set out top trades for various macro scenarios given the wide dispersion in client views...

• ...Recession risk is well-priced in lower-yielding FX but not among high-yielders which are priced to softlanding. Scandi FX, AUD are viable soft landing candidates. Prior high-yielders where yields are equalizing with USD to be penalized.

Other authors for this report include: Ben Jarman,Junya Tanase, Antonin Delair, Octavia Popescu, Kunj Padh, Ikue Saito, Joyce Lai and Tiffany Wang.

## A quick look back

In our 2024 year-ahead FX outlook titled This still isn’t over, our baseline was for USD to be bumpy but elevated. Our preference was to buy the dollar on dips but with the recognition that one needs to be tactical as markets vacillate between US exceptionalism and recession scenarios. Our top tradable themes were: (1) synchronized central bank rate cuts and the related global yield compression which would result in global FX carry eventually running out of steam and allow for pockets of valuation convergence (we were long SEK vs. CZK in this regard), (2) central bank/ recession sequencing (we had recommended long AUD and SEK vs. short GBP and CAD) and (3) Euro underperformance (short EUR/USD, EUR/CHF) but with some exposure to possible soft landing (short EUR/SEK) and US recession (cost-effective USD/JPY put) outcomes.

Six months on, the view has been panned out for the most part but with some surprises as well. What has worked is that the dollar has remained strong in 1H even though the drivers have changed (1Q was about the Fed re-pricing from >6 cuts in 2024 to just 2; 2Q was about volatility shocks from EM elections / carry trade washout). Synchronized rate cuts materialized amid a broad downshift in inflation, with 43% of central banks having cut rates already in 2Q. The Fed has notably lagged this cutting cycle which is atypical since it usually is a forerunner in easing in the last four decades (Figure 2). This not only improved rate differentials in favour of the dollar, but also eroded the yield cushion on global carry baskets making the high-yielders progressively more vulnerable to vol shocks, as became evident in early June. As yield differentials have shrunk, the footprint of carry as driver of FX returns has grown smaller, and other factors have emerged as more relevant - growth, inflation and valuation

signals for instance have started to matter as well, unlike carry’s overriding dominance in 2023 (Figure 3Globa cryemnw thpd 203i  s; 1   g k v u). G10 was an exception to the yield compression theme as the high yielders didn’t cut, and this allowed G10 carry to buck the global carry trend and deliver solid returns, but central bank sequencing and size of easing still ended up informing relative G10 currency returns (Figure 4Relativ cnrbk -pgs mdfo G10FX YTD,wh JP u).

![](images/2ede98153b0c9aca03bf4b45f158e39afa230dc087b9e060a7e3ae84796fd703.jpg)

More broadly, the main macro surprises in 1H24 were (1) more resilient growth across countries which led to several upgrades in 1H (UK led this in G10, as did the US and European countries); recession ended up not really a theme in most countries; and (2) fewer upside surprises in inflation but with US inflation surprising the most to the upside in DM (rest of the countries were mostly softer-to-in line; Figure 5). Indeed, a key takeaway for markets in H1 was that disinflation in absolute, and downshift in inflation expectations are two different things: because the former is widely factored into economist / consensus forecasts, it is much less influential than the latter for driving rates / FX. In this vein, the 0.5% rise in the JPM US core CPI FRI explains why UST yields are \~50bp higher and DXY \~4.5% stronger YTD despite US core CPI falling from 3.9% to 3.4% oya since the turn of the year – a lesson to keep in mind as we head into H2 anticipating a continued CPI softening and associated monetary easing expectations that are already well discounted into forward OIS curves.

Among the views on the major currencies, EUR and CNY did weaken modestly vs. USD since we published in November; AUD ended up outperforming within G10 as was our expectation; NOK also joined this resilient bucket of lateeasers which was incorporated into our view in 1H. Among the laggards, the bearish CAD view played out as they cut rates pre-Fed. However, our bearish GBP view which was predicated on expectations of a UK recession was challenged as was our bearish view on NZD; both G10 high-yielders benefitted instead from sticky inflation which kept their central banks hawkish. Meanwhile, JPY was the biggest casualty to higher US rates and the glacial shift in BoJ policy; we held a bearish yen bias in 1H even though our targets were tame (153), but the 2H turn in USD/JPY in anticipation of lower

US yields and a BoJ pivot is no longer expected.

Figure 5: The main 1H24 macro surprises were (1) more resilient growth across many countries and (2) US inflation surprising the most to the upside in DM

6m change in our economists growth and inflation forecast revision indices; %pt

![](images/9f5e96b1b7d1ba027f16d35f3610510c05cb0317e410f2847dcf0071caa98ddc.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

The view: Strong USD, narrow smile, focus on US elections...

Our view looks for the dollar to stay elevated (expressed via EUR and CNH puts vs. USD), even setting aside the possible impact from the US elections. The bullish USD view continues to be motivated by the same two pillars which continue to stay intact to varying degrees. First, the dollar’s improving yield advantage as other central banks cut rates before and by more than the Fed. Second, US exceptionalism continues to linger even with US growth moderating as recovery in Eurozone and China remains tepid, if not fragile. As in 1H, trading approach remains tactical. Pockets of USD weakness will exist but the valley will be narrow and is best played through the mid-yielders (think Scandis, AUD in G10) that are likely to benefit from cheap valuations and as the very high yielders gradually fall out of favour due to crowded positioning, rich valuations and declining yields. A Fed pause/ modest easing by itself wouldn’t be sufficient for outsized USD weakness; the latter would require a growth pick up outside of the US as well. Even though this isn’t our base case, we remain highly vigilant for any outcome that entails a combination of these two conditions given rich USD valuations.

Risks to the USD skew bullishly from the US elections. We already see evidence that US elections are subtly influencing price action; this can grow in 2H. Key channels of transmission for currencies are trade and tariff risk (USD positive), Ukraine / Russia policy (EUR-negative), fiscal (USD positive if policy supports growth) and dollar policy (could be longer term USD-bearish). Among these, the first two could be

implemented in a timelier manner with unilateral Presidential enforcement. Election trades: From a tactical perspective, we see value in medium-term USD/CNH digital risk reversals (see Derivatives below). On EUR, we are inclined to reset downside exposure but will assess after the passage of this weekend’s French election risk. Finally, election event risk pricing in options is broadly tracking prior cycles though with some sub-surface variations: USD TWI and CNH O/N vols are close to 2016 and 2020 election cycle highs, MXN is middling but has room to rally, while EUR/USD and other European FX are visibly lagging prior cycle pricing.

## 1. USD: What is the top down view for the dollar?

Our view looks for the dollar to stay elevated (bullish view expressed currently through EUR and CNH puts vs. USD), even setting aside the possible impact from the US elections (latter is addressed separately in Q2 below). As in 1H, trading approach remains tactical. The bullish USD view had been motivated by two pillars which continue to stay intact to varying degrees. First, the dollar’s improving yield advantage, especially considering that it is a defensive currency. Even though the Fed has been on hold, in the global context the dollar’s yield advantage is actually improving as other central banks have been cutting rates. Specifically, the dollar is currently yielding more than 44% of the global universe (Figure 6) with 34% of central banks having already cut rates. That other central banks have pre-empted the Fed should keep US yield spreads elevated in favour of the dollar (Figure 1). Figure 1 lso shows an alternate scenario in which only the Fed delivers four more cuts than expected by our economists, assuming that the outlook for all other central banks remain unchanged, and illustrates that if realized, it will barely make a dent in the dollar’s yield advantage. In reality, if the Fed ends up delivering more rate cuts than currently projected, other central banks will cut more as well and hence the US rate spreads will likely realize higher than that shown in these charts.

Figure 6: The dollar will still yield more than 50% of the currencies globally if the Fed delivers five cuts by end-2025; it’s ranking would fall if four additional Fed cuts were delivered...

% of currencies yielding less than the dollar. Blue dotted line uses JPM Economics forecast of 1 cut in 2024 and 4 in 2025; red is an alternative Fed scenario with 9 cuts to end-2025 with no change to other central bank policy rates.

![](images/f7f3a508bfd45cabeb3c3dfae604bca5cc82cb6d3cae6a4ab5667edf75144c33.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan
Figure 7: ...but aggregate US yield differential support would still remain elevated

US - RoW median policy rate (%) vs USD TWI. Blue dotted line uses JPM Economics forecasts; red is an alternative Fed scenario with 9 cuts to end-2025
![](images/5b804003a40a4f2da1c764a8134083b07f6e4ed813520cbc29c4c0804d1a9dcf.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

Second, US growth exceptionalism was the additional pillar to our USD bullish view. This pillar has de-intensified since the start of the year as US growth has moderated (from 3.4%ar in 4Q23 to 1.7% in 1H24), while the outlook for Europe has improved and that for China has stabilized. However, some elements of US exceptionalism still linger. For instance, Eurozone June PMIs showed that the momentum may be already running out of steam heading into 3Q, while China growth continues to be feeble and unlikely to receive a meaningful fillip from the upcoming 3<sup>rd</sup> Plenum (link). Moreover, monetary policy divergence has also started to unfold with the ECB already cutting rates and PBoC remaining dovish. Finally, our commodity strategists are bullish, which if realized will benefit the dollar more than commodity importers. EUR/USD target near-term target is 1.05 and USD/ CNY at 7.30 with upside risks.

There is the broader question of whether the dollar can continue to strengthen if US rates have already peaked. We think it can if policy divergences between the Fed and the RoW get more pronounced or if we get more vol shocks. For context, our rates strategists think it unlikely that the highs in US yields from May 2024 will be re-visited, and given how highly correlated the dollar has been to the level of US yields, there is a view held among some that lower yields should automatically result in a weaker dollar. However, as Figure 1shows, US rate differentials in aggregate should continue to remain relatively elevated in favour of the dollar even if the Fed ends up cutting more than we expect, and in any case is already discounted to significant degree in forward OIS curves. Any USD strength related to a hawkish Fed repricing/ US exceptionalism/ further policy divergence as in 1Q should weigh more on funders while a downshift in global growth / vol shocks will result in carry unwinds similar to that in June which will weigh more on high yielders vs. USD (Figure 8). The dollar smile still exists in our view. Indeed, pockets of USD weakness will exist but the valley will be narrow and is best played through the mid-yielders (think Scandis, AUD in G10) that are likely to benefit from cheap valuations and as the very high yielders gradually fall out of favour due to crowded positioning, rich valuations and declining yields.

Figure 8: The dollar smile exists: Any USD strength related to a hawkish Fed re-pricing as in 1Q should weigh more on funders; a downshift in global growth/ vol shocks weigh more on the high yielders.

% chg in CCY/USD from 29<sup>th</sup> December to 17<sup>th</sup> May and 17<sup>th</sup> May to 21<sup>st</sup> June.

![](images/afad7c760d780638c6aaccac318ccbde0539948a63ae671f2c62c08a2ab8a0e2.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

What is the set of circumstances that can result in a 5% or larger weakening in USD? It has been our long-held view that a Fed pause/ modest easing by itself wouldn’t be sufficient for outsized USD weakness; the latter would require

a growth pick up outside of the US as well. While either one of these conditions have been met from time-to-time (expectations of large Fed cuts in 4Q23; growth improvements outside the US in 2Q24), both have yet to be met together. Even though this isn’t our base case, we remain highly vigilant for any outcome that entails a combination of these two conditions, as one of our key learnings from 2023 was that the market is more eager to position for dollar weakness than for dollar strength given long-term US fiscal concerns and rich valuations. This was also evident in price action in 2023: vol of DXY up days has on average been smaller than that on down days. Our take-away remains that if growth outside the US is turning up, one should be quick to reduce dollar longs.

## 2. Is there anything left to trade for the US elections?

We introduced our original framework for thinking about the impacts of US elections on FX markets in November, and maintain that risks to the USD skew bullishly into the event (see US Elections & FX, P. Locke et al). In that note, we evaluated the key channels of transmission for currencies (Figure 9), which include:

Trade and tariff risk (USD positive via growth/competitiveness & anti-cyclical channels; unilateral enforcement)

Ukraine / Russia policy (thought to be eventually EURnegative if geopolitical vulnerabilities worsen; unilateral enforcement)

Fiscal (USD positive if policy supports growth; mediumterm question marks for USD on deficit implications)

FX policy (intervention negative for USD but likely ineffective; bigger potential impacts from Fed policy if invoked)

Our key takeaways from that original analysis are that risk premium in FX should be concentrated in tariff risk reflecting 1) empirical sensitivities seen during Pres. Trump’s first administration, and 2) the ability to implement policy (largely) unilaterally whereas fiscal policy requires Congress in most cases. On net we believe the USD TWI could appreciate 5% if 10% universal baseline tariffs were to be implemented. We have since elaborated on implications for EUR from geopolitical considerations, including from potential policy shifts around the Russia/ Ukraine war (see Key Currency Views, May 2024). A ceasefire in Ukraine could be considered by markets as EUR-positive at least in knee jerk since it introduces prospects for Europe to get access to cheap energy again; but beyond this initial reaction, the durability of this positive sentiment would once again be questionable. It is also worth considering that larger defense spending could also be growth-positive. Along with trade, the range of policy pass-throughs to EUR could be wide, and so warrant elevated vol premium in EUR/USD.

Figure 9: Risks are skewed bullishly for USD from the electora process next year if certain policies come into play, led by tariff risk Best estimates of potential impact on USD across policies. Includes base-case range if implemented, with potential max/min scenarios. %

![](images/8a8f8978f92bb58b05430c0a698cdfa12c7c3d1d7efc43ce61a5fe5cba51377a.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

Why hasn’t elections impacted FX more obviously so far this year? There is perhaps some consternation that elections have not been ‘tradable’ this year despite broad market interest. We can point to several reasons for this. First, some popular positions (short CNH, short EUR) are already held for other macro reasons – suggesting no urgent need to reorient portfolios. Second, the USD TWI has largely tracked US rates this year, reflecting the uncertainty around the trajectory of US inflation and Fed expectations – a more immediate concern in 1H than the more-distant Nov elections.

But we do see evidence that US elections are subtly influencing price action; this can grow in 2H. We had previously shown USD TWI risk premia correlating to proxies of tariff risk (see FXMW); that correlation has been muddied however following Pres. Biden’s recent tariff announcement, as well as the EM/European-linked vol event earlier this month. But we see other signs of FX sensitivity to the event. First, certain election-related vol metrics continue tracking poll margins, and forward vol curves continue to screen steep for more vulnerable currencies. Elsewhere, we note that some measures of USD/CNH FV mispricing are tracking elections polls, which is consistent with risks of tariff policy risks (Figure 10) - particularly now that the CNY fix is easing up a bit. All together, this suggests FX is striking a balance between immediate macro developments and evolving election risks. As such, we would highlight several developments since our original publication that are FX-relevant:

1. Some polls and probability markets have moved more in favor of Pres Trump recently (Figure 11), which we

think should help keep a floor under USD in 2H’24 given tariff and trade policy risks.

2. There seems to be increasing interest in fiscal outcomes, reflecting 1) ongoing uncertainty around Congressional composition, 2) the large deficit implications from TCJA (now estimated around \$4trn over 10Y if fully extended) and 3) more new news on fiscal rather than trade (lower taxes on tips, a marginal shift lower in the corporate tax rate). Fiscal will matter for FX but we maintain that tariff risk is paramount.

3. Positioning adjustments and valuations. Our objective screener had originally flagged MXN as vulnerable given its outsized trade relationship to the US, but also because of rich valuations and long positioning. The latter two have normalized relative to earlier 1H’24 levels. Similarly, CNH shorts have increased, as have EUR, CAD & JPY (this compares to the short-USD position in 4Q’23 from our original publication). All together, the USD is already up 5% this year, to the highest levels since 4Q’22, meaning pricing election risk premium will not get tailwinds from positioning or valuations.

## 3. Should the rotation of away from global carry continue? What other factors will be relevant for FX?

## Global FX carry: unattractive risk-reward

FX carry was once again the top performing strategy from January to mid-May this year. Returns were mostly driven by short positions in low yielders (JPY, CHY and EM Asia), and the final stretch of HY EM strength in the likes of MXN. However, in the past month, Global and EM carry baskets posted significant losses due to EM FX collapse following elections across the globe, exacerbated by crowded positioning. On the other hand, G10 carry is still holding with +10% returns YTD (Figure 13).

Figure 13: G10 carry is strongly outperforming EM carry where returns have been wiped out

Ytd total return index of Global/EM/G10 nominal carry baskets (top-bottom 5/3/2).

![](images/c7327c7319bbf73f60028520227e8c11e044a3d580fe0c2a3aec393a0c5ec932.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

The main question now is: how to think about FX carry after this significant downward move? The bottom-line is that FX carry is far from the levels of attractiveness of 2023 and does not offer good risk-reward considering (i) impending US election noise and (ii) the risk of the Fed delivering more than expected cuts. The key concern is that the era of synchronized rate cuts led by the very highest yielders has compressed the yields one can earn on global carry baskets (Figure 14) and thus reducing their ability to withstand vol shocks. The outlook on global FX carry thus remains vulnerable. While it is entirely possible for some returns from EM carry to be recouped selectively as vol dissipates, the longer-term outlook is still eventually going to be clouded by the lack of yield on carry baskets, which means that the cushion against vol shocks remains thin. Among the very high yielders, our EM team is overweight INR and MXN. DM carry (long USD, GBP; short CHF, JPY) is a wholly different animal and should remain resilient given wide yields (Figure 14) so long as inflation remains sticky and also informs our view that the dollar will likely remain well-supported at elevated levels as well.

## A status quo in 2H24 should extend the current G10 drivers

During the first part of the year, the divergence between G10 and EM drivers has been significant (Figure 15). This has been because rate cuts in EM opened the door for other signals to become more relevant (like value, growth, commodity terms of trade momentum). Meanwhile, in G10 FX, FX price action was informed primarily by carry as no rate cuts were forthcoming from the highest yielders. A legitimate question going forward then is: will this factor rotation observed in EM also happen for G10 in 2H24 or the divergences between DM central banks are still too significant?

Our bias is to say that there is not enough priced by the Fed to significantly trigger this factor rotation in G10 yet. The yield gap between the top 2/bottom 2 G10 high yielders is forecasted to stay above 4%, which is high historically (even with

three Fed cuts which would make GBP and NZD the top two yielders in G10 instead of USD and NZD; see FX Macro Quant).

Source: J.P. Morgan

That said, the current spectrum of possible Fed outcomes remains relatively large (from 1 to 3 cuts). Despite the G10 yield dispersion staying high, a very dovish (and unexpected) Fed outcome could trigger this rotation. In such case, the most relevant drivers of FX would be: (i) long term value as cheap G10s would reprice on US rates relief; (ii) rates momentum as the different trajectories between DM central banks could become a driver if the Fed delivers multiple cuts (instead of the absolute yield gap; i.e. carry) and (iii) to a lesser extent, relative growth.

## 4. Where is a soft landing well-priced? What trades to consider for other macro scenarios?

In our year-ahead outlook, we noted that recession risk was well-priced into low-yielding FX but not among highyielders (the latter are well priced to a soft-landing); this remains the case (Figure 16). The figure shows that the broad USD index has already strengthened by the average move usually seen in a recession; however, this masks the composition of this strengthening which has mostly come against the lowest yielding currencies. A glance through the rest of the chart indicates that broadly speaking, it’s the funders that have weakened quite a bit already while the high yielders are rich, a clear manifestation of the carry trade that has been in vogue in the last two years. Our expectation has been that as carry converges, so should valuations. In G10, Scandi FX and AUD still screen weaker, or in line with their usual recession performance and are thus promising soft landing/recovery candidates; so long as global yield convergence unfolds. In EM, ZAR and INR stand out as cheap on this metric; our EM strategy colleagues have reinstated their bullish ZAR bias post-election and are long INR as well so these are notable long candidates. In contrast, the very highest-yielders are stronger than their average recessionary downturn (NZD in G10, MXN, BRL, IDR in EM), largely as a consequence of strong support from carry over the last two years. This would arguably make the HY bloc vulnerable in a global downturn. Among the standout rich currencies, our EM strategists have recently moved UW COP and maintain their bearish CZK bias.

Markets are still not fully convinced which way the global business cycle is going, so we set out Top Trades for various scenarios. Our global economists regularly update their probability tree for global outlook scenarios (link) which include two variations of their ‘boil the frog’ and two types of soft landing. In the ‘Top Trades to consider’ figure below we look at the range of global growth and bond yield combinations through a 3x3 grid and highlight top trades. The extremes are easiest to set out: growth and bond yields either at the high or low end of the range together create a variation of risk-on/off outcomes. Top left and bottom right quadrant are the best and worst outcomes for the FX carry trades respectively. Yield convergence trades (top right and middle right quadrants) if US yields were to fall significantly are of great interest to us as that’s where the most favourable risk/ reward is (short GBP/SEK, NZD/NOK are attractive examples).

If growth slows but yields stay in a range (bottom middle quadrant) and equities therefore are at risk of correction, we think JPY struggles to perform as a risk hedge given

Global FX Strategy FX Mid-Year Outlook 28 June 2024

BoJ policy divergence. Short cross CHF (vs SEK, EUR) would be preferred to long JPY on slower growth (bottom row). If growth moderates but yields head back to the highs (middle left quadrant) then CHF/JPY should do well. If growth accelerates and yields stay in a range (middle top bucket) then we like buying GBP on dips, such as fading any BoE easing inspired sterling weakness. We like using CAD as a proxy dollar for example if yields fall and growth improves (top right quadrant) then short CAD vs high beta is an effective short USD strategy. If yields fall more aggressively than we expect (right hand column) then Scandis and AUD should outperform high yielders on RV as they were used as beta neutral hedges for EM carry. In the central bucket, if yields stay rangebound and growth settles into a moderate but not too impressive state, then the focus will likely be on RV trades such as long NOK/SEK and AUD/NZD.

## 5. Is central bank divergence still a G10 theme?

Yes. Central bank policy is converging as all central banks but BoJ are expected to cut rates. However, the differentiating impact on FX will come from relative start / sequencing and size of easing. Forward rate dispersion across G10 has picked up relative to early 2024, while remaining well below the peaks seen in 2023 (Figure 17). This is natural as rate hikes are now off the table for most CBs, but rate cut expectations have also been chastened amid a resilient global growth backdrop. Naturally, such prospects for CB divergence have also been very derivative of Fed expectations: a deeper/earlier Fed cutting cycle reduced dispersion from over 4Q23 as all central banks were presumed to cut hard (the high yielders more so). Pricing of a slower global easing cycle since Jan ’24 has meant that forward rate differentials must reflect spot differentials to a greater degree, and thus impact FX.

![](images/46d8e6b75877f55f9ad58ab0955d4f7f3c3e1f6a0e874c1fdfc454691e3e3746.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

Domestic vs. external focused monetary policy orientation is a potential RV dimension. Figure 18 shows that the Fed is expected to be a shallow cutter in the global context and hence the dollar’s yield advantage will not erode easily. Meanwhile, lower yielders like SEK and AUD are pointing in different directions near-term. A conceptual grouping of central banks might be: those who are managing to local conditions (CAD and SEK dovishly, AUD hawkishly), and those which are potentially more focused on global inflation trends or constrained by the Fed itself (NOK, RBNZ, to an extent BoE and ECB).

So far this year, those who are managing to local conditions in a dovish direction (CAD, SEK) are already underperforming in absolute terms, while the comparatively hawkish AUD has outperformed relative to changes in rate pricing. Expectations for GBP and EUR easing have been pared most this year, though this has not been a clear arbiter of those pairs’ performance, reflecting constraints and relative growth news. Hawkish repricing has also not been supportive for the RBNZ or Norges Bank consistently, who have maintained a tough love approach but at some cost to growth, so bringing little FX dividend considering the YTD rate repricing.

Figure 19: Most central banks will be easing over the next year, but the size of cuts lends itself to RV

Y: expected change in policy rates from peak (from current for JPY) by 2Q’25; X: projected level of policy rate by 2Q’25. JPM Economics forecasts.

![](images/8e85ba3c253c5f053bcec5660b64e692a831258ee281529b16ca03568e44b1d8.jpg)

![](images/098a19907f9fbe61c20bffce5c3eaed803731ee1bea7ece4290b8f9b9e03a86c.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

External constraints on easing imply that high-for-long real rates have adverse growth effects and are not automatically FX positive. Our forecast of consistent but only moderate Fed easing from 4Q should mean policy constraints remain for some of the latter category of central banks, unless domestic inflation comes to heel. On this point, USD, NZD and NOK stand out as economies where we expect real rates to remain high through 4Q and where CPI revisions has been more up than down. Growth weakness and the likelihood of a rollover in %oya inflation in 2H make it hard for NZD to wear real rates as a badge of honour though (compared to say NOK), and we expect capitulation from the RBNZ in 2H24. GBP by contrast has an elevated real rate structure (Figure 20 ) but has also seen downside surprises in CPI which could see a more dovish pivot on global factors. AUD inflation revi-

sions have been lower of late, but from a quite different baseline where the central bank is only attempting to return to the target band in 2025/26, so keeps alive the hiking bias nearterm.

Bottom line is that sequencing of easing opens up potential for policy RV which involves long USD vs. the likes of EUR, NZD and CAD for instance, or vs. various high beta EM FX which are are expected fall into dollar yield territory (see Figure 17). USD aside, this also lends itself to other RV such as long AUD/NZD or long NOK/SEK, both of which we continue to recommend. The G10 inflation outlooks suggests a more supportive stance for AUD, USD, GBP and NOK vs. the likes of SEK, NZD and CAD; watch EUR where inflation will stay sticky and could limit easing (Figure 21The G10inflato uksg mrpv c AUD,SBP dNOK.  EZ C;w R y ).

The final dimension to consider with policy sequencing is that it will be hard for this driver to completely differentiate FX returns as certain self-supporting factors will also come into play for early cutters diluting the policy divergence impact. For instance, the growth advantages of policy easing will also accrue sooner in cycle for rate cutters which should eventually help buffet the impact of policy easing. SEK is one example of where we expect such a dynamic to unfold.

## 6. CNY FX: Is the most consensus US election hedge still the best one for H2?

The consensus bearish CNY view coming into the year was challenging to monetize in 1H24. USD/CNH is \~2% higher YTD, which is an anemic outturn given structural domestic growth headwinds and a more challenging external backdrop. CNY TWI has actually risen \~1% this year even as the DXY has rallied, a testament to PBoC’s heavy management of FX weakness; indeed an aggregate index of various market-based PBoC intervention proxies suggests that this is now the most interventionist the central bank has been since the 2015/’16 devaluation episode, markedly more so than at the height of 2018/’19 trade conflict (Figure 22). Early signs heading into H2 is that this is starting to shift a little at the margin judging by the recent creep higher in the CNY fixings, presumably as authorities look to release some pent-up depreciation pressures now in return for greater headroom to manage FX volatility later, if required.

Figure 22: Based on market-based proxies of PBoC intervention, this is the most hands-on the central bank has been on FX since the 2015/’16 devaluation episode

z-scores of various intervention proxies (monthly change in PBoC’s FX outstanding, gap between USD/CNH and CNY fix; CNH-CNY implied yield gap, spread between 12m Shibor and 1y CNY implied yield)

![](images/fdd3a7b5f77d595f5e55a261dea806879d52ef3f088cba9fdd4c1a179f26c5fd.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P., J.P. Morgan

H2 is likely to bring geopolitical risks facing CNY to the fore more forcefully. Trump’s proposed 60%+ tariff on Chinese imports is likely to be the principal determinant of investor sentiment around CNY through the heat of the US election campaign season. Mechanically, dusting off our tariff-neutral framework from the 2018/’19 period that explained USD/ CNY movements reasonably well suggests levels close to 9.0 on USD/CNY using the old beta of \~0.7% CNY depreciation for every 1% increase in the average tariff rate (Figure 23). However this is likely a (substantial) overestimate of tariff effects on CNY FX since: (a) China’s exchange rate elasticity of exports has fallen markedly over the years as it has moved up the manufacturing value chain; (b) PBoC will almost certainly not allow FX to be the principal shock absorber on this scale this time around unlike in the past; and (c) large scale de-risking of foreigners’ exposure to Chinese assets since 2022 likely takes some of the sting off punitive levies.

Figure 23: USD/CNY and the tariff-neutral framework
![](images/e09ac9f3da67f41b5a0379ecb0bb6e77a33e97f6fb2ba68a4ffc75af252a453c.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

Paucity of geopolitical risk premium likely to keep CNY depreciation intact. Despite weaker pass-through from tariffs to CNY FX, the sheer scale of the potential shock merits a high degree of caution around the currency, which is intensified by the starting point of near-zero risk premium in USD/ CNY over and above that warranted by relative to US-CN yield differentials. Our year-end forecast of 7.30 for the pair is less a reflection of cyclical and tail risks around the currency, and more a call on PBoC’s FX management behavior; needless to say that the balance of risks around this projection is biased squarely to the upside absent a material fall in UST yields. How far could USD/CNY realistically rally in an adverse scenario? Applying a peak Covid-era risk premium on top of rate differential based fair value gets one to \~7.50. Alternatively, the full loss of exports to the US is worth \~\$100bn reduction in China’s trade surplus, which is worth \~4% NEER weakness and implies USD/CNY \~7.60. Finally, a simple time series history of rolling 6-mo returns suggests that the PBoC’s tolerance for CNY depreciation tends to be capped at \~10% for USD/CNH and 6% for the CNY TWI, which implies lines in the sand \~8.0 for USD/CNY and \~92 for CNY TWI. So net-net, an adverse case for CNY could see it weaken to 7.6-7.7 vs. USD, which should keep macro investors interested in H2 over and above the forward point carry on offer.

Shifts in intervention strategy worth watching if/as FX stresses intensify. The bulk of official resistance to CNY weakness has thus far been effected by state bank supply of dollars, alongside the use of CNY fixings and periodic tightening of CNH liquidity to flush out speculative RMB shorts. H2 could see a richer toolkit for currency management emerge if/as depreciation pressures intensify. The PBoC could unleash is own FXR dollars that have been by and large been left unused so far as a part of a long-term strategy to veer away from direct intervention and preserve some reserve adequacy elbow room for times of acute stress. Additionally, excess dollar savings of Chinese corporates worth \$200- 550bn (based on the FX conversion assumption used) that have been relatively untapped so far could be used for firefighting (Figure 24). Finally, tighter capital account outflow restrictions and macroprudential measures like FX reserve ratio cuts can also be rolled out.

## 7. Will FX vol pick up materially in H2?

Not really; some US election related noise is inevitable, but a clean trend higher in FX vol is unlikely.

FX vol went nowhere fast in H1. Outside of the late Q2 washout in EM FX carry, FX volatility was by and large out of commission through H1 as we had generally anticipated coming into the year (FX Vol: Not going anywhere fast). The ebbing of rate volatility contributed meaningfully to lackluster FX vols, as the late’23 optimism around muscular rate cuts gave way to the reality of policy inaction for the most part in the face of sticky inflation.

Moderately cheap FX vol + risk-supportive cyclicals = rangebound VXY. VXY Global is entering 2H24 with a familiar undervalued feel. VXY Global screens 1.2 - 1.5 % pts. contemporaneously low when benchmarked to PMIs and rates vol (Figure 25). We are not convinced that a disruptive correction higher in volatility is in the offing in H2 given the overall risk supportive global growth backdrop (PMIs > 50), a broader cohort of central banks embarking on monetary easing, and USD positions already substantially long. More tactically, seasonals are not particularly favorable for FX vol through the first half of Q3 before turning more constructive post the summer lull. However fresh cyclical lows in vol is also unlikely, given that assets are generally rich to the state of the global cycle, which means that there is no cyclical risk premium to be squeezed out from financial markets via disinflationary Fed cuts. More importantly, noise around the US election is likely to keep markets choppy in the lead-up, and O/N vols for the event will likely grind higher as it draws nearer, especially in undervalued pockets like EUR.

FXO trading themes for H2 are as follows: (a) preference for gamma over convexity (GBP/USD 1M ATM vs. 10D

strangles in equals); (b) fading late-Q2 carry trade washout / French election related risk premium (3M USD/MXN 1\*2 ratio put spreads, -1M/+2M downside one-touch calendar spreads and short EUR correlations); (c) US elections pricing is tracking for MXN, looks subdued in EUR (-4M / +8M SEK and HUF vol calendars), and is continuing to drift higher for CNH (9M USD/CHF digital riskies); (e) cross-Yen RVs (USD/JPY 155/150 inverse ratio put spreads; USD/JPY vs. CHF/JPY vol RV; USD/JPY - USD/NOk dual digitals); and (f) contingency hedges for unforeseen DXY weakness (low delta USD/EM digitals in TRY, MXN, BRL)and equity weakness (inverse ratio put spreads in AUD/CHF, GBP/JPY).

Figure 25: FX vols screen cheap when benchmarked to macro growth and rate vol
![](images/563f0f0bde773acdeb9b7d7ade0426ce94b137b439512626cbbf7b65fcd7ab88.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P., J.P.Morgan

## 8. What is the outlook on funders?

## Tactically short funders with risks from yields and elections

We come out of the mid-year process with a view to be tactically short funders, but more for idiosyncratic bottom-up reasons rather than a broad top down view and with some differentiation.USD/JPY has disconnected from rate spreads and given the uncertainties surrounding the BoJ policy, no intention of the MoF to defend any certain levels of USD/JPY, and carry seeking flows (even if the face of narrowing rate spreads), it is not clear to us that the time is right to be long JPY yet. With a cyclically positive view on growth still intact at the global level, we are more minded to be short JPY versus cyclical currencies such as AUD, NOK and GBP on pullbacks in those crosses. Indeed we have just taken profit on our AUD/JPY long this week (link) and see any JPY rallies driven by possible MoF intervention as an opportunity to re-enter JPY shorts. For CHF, the currency has the strongest seasonality of all global FX in June, and combined with heightened European political risk and SNB governor Jordan’s hawkish speech earlier in the month, EUR/

Patrick R Locke (1-212) 834-4254 patrick.r.locke@jpmchase.com J.P. Morgan Securities LLC James Nelligan (44-20) 3493-6829 james.nelligan@jpmorgan.com J.P. Morgan Securities plc

CHF fell from above 0.99 to below 0.95 at one point. As we exit June, our bias is for the political risk premium in CHF to reduce and more normal seasonality trends to allow CHF to weaken over the short term horizon. Beyond that things become more tricky for the franc, as the last round of Europe an PMIs underperformed, the SNB isn’t as dovish as it once was, tariff and FX policy risks come into view, and the Fed approaches policy easing. So look for some tactical CHF weakness in July, but depending on how growth and yields evolve after that, there could be an entry point for CHF longs.

CHF vs JPY - some similarities but core differences During H1-24 we argued that CHF and JPY were being driven by similar forces (link), but as we get further into H2-24 their differences should become more apparent. CHF and JPY occupy the bottom two rankings in G10 in terms of yield volatility, real carry and risk-adjusted carry, as well as having among the most negative risk beta in G10 (Figure 27). During H1-24 that made them ideal funders for the currencies of economies where growth was accelerating. It also increased their sensitivity to US yields. For investors debating whether markets were in the middle or the right side of the dollar smile, being short CHF and JPY provided some asymmetry in that they could weaken versus the USD in both of those environments.

Looking at the respective Balance of Payments, Switzerland ran a -4.2% of GDP basic balance in Q1, which is rare for a country that is used to solid BoP surpluses. Japan ran a 1.7% of GDP basic balance. Both positions are dragged down by FDI and debt flows (Figure 26). Recent FDI outflows from Switzerland came as a strong currency encouraged Swiss companies to carry out more M&A. The FDI trend in Japan however has been much more persistent over time, and has come as Japanese companies have tried to ‘buy growth abroad’ and taken advantage of historically cheaper labour and production costs overseas. The debt outflow on both BoPs speaks to the carry-seeking world we are now in, as yield differentials remain wide. Even in a world where rate spreads remain constant, the fair value for USD/JPY in practice likely still rises as these debt outflows occur. Of course debt outflows are usually run with higher hedge ratios than equity outflows, but local institutions have been lowering hedge ratios (link).

Ultimately, CHF has traded as more of a safe haven than JPY in our view this year, and that’s the core difference between the two currencies as far as funding characteristics go. Should a rise in US yields weaken equity markets, then CHF is more likely to respond positively than JPY, we think. The larger size of CHF cross border funding compared to JPY (Figure 27) would speak to that conclusion. Furthermore, the

SNB has more influence over CHF than BoJ does over JPY, in our view, with for example SNB governor Jordan’s recent hawkish speech having an outsized impact on CHF compared to the diminishing impact of BoJ and MoF pushback on JPY weakness. Should US yields rise in H2-24 we think JPY would clearly underform CHF, and vice versa on lower yields.

JPY: Bearish, but the scale of H1 weakness won’t repeat as relative monetary cycles turn

Since the launch of Fed rate hike cycle in March 2022, US monetary policy expectation have been the main driver of USD/JPY.Given the strong correlation between USD/JPY and FF futures, our USD/JPY target as of end-September this year is 157, which is in line with one cut by the Fed in 2024. Our economic research team expects one cut by the Fed and two hikes by the BoJ within this year and divergence in monetary policy between the US and Japan to continue. This divergence should lead a modest decline in USD/JPY in the medium-term

Meera Chandan <sup>AC</sup> (44-20) 7134-2924 meera.chandan@jpmorgan.com J.P. Morgan Securities plc Arindam Sandilya <sup>AC</sup> (65) 6882-7759 arindam.x.sandilya@jpmorgan.com JPMorgan Chase Bank, N.A., Singapore Bran

Patrick R Locke (1-212) 834-4254 patrick.r.locke@jpmchase.com J.P. Morgan Securities LLC James Nelligan (44-20) 3493-6829 james.nelligan@jpmorgan.com

Given the recent rally in USD/JPY to above 160, the highest since 1986, however, the pair has now deviated from the level suggested by the past correlation with FF futures (Figure 28). One possible explanation for the recent deviation of USD/ JPY from rate expectations is booming JPY carry trades. For carry trades, absolute levels of short-term interest rate spreads and its sustainability are key factors, while long-term yields driven by monetary policy expectations become less significant for FX rate developments. The current attractiveness of JPY carry trades is comparable to that seen in 2006- 07, when the JPY carry trade was booming (Figure 29). Also, history suggests that the long USD/JPY carry trade tends to remain well supported so long as the US-JP yield spread is above 4% (Figure 30).

More fundamentally, Japan’s deeply negative real interest rate have driven underlying JPY weakness in recent years, and there is some speculation that domestic outflows from Japan could even accelerate at some point, even as the Fed / BoJ policy gap begins to narrow. Our baseline scenario is that of a modest decline in USD/JPY driven by monetary policy divergence, but recent developments suggest the possibility of an alternative scenario — outright JPY weakness driven by carry trades and/or domestic capital flight — has materially risen.

Anxiety around the MoF intervention and/or actual intervention is liable to cap USD/JPY upside to some extent. The key determinants of FX intervention decisions are the speed of Yen movement and the market context rather than any specific levels. When JPY-buying intervention was implemented on April 29, USD/JPY’s rise from the previous day’s low had exceeded 5 yen; hence if USD/JPY rises by 4-5 yen within 1-2 business days, the possibility of another round of JPY-buying intervention cannot be ruled out. Additionally, the threshold for intervention might be slightly lower this time around than it was at the end of April, given the larger deviation of USD/JPY vs. UST yields and US-JP yield spreads (note).

## CHF - Tactically bearish, things become more difficult deeper into H2

In our end-May note titled “Short term obstacles to medium term CHF weakness” (link) we said June would be harder to see CHF weaken given strong seasonality, SNB governor Jordan’s hawkish speech and the June SNB meeting. On top of that we got a surprise French election which pushed CHF stronger as well. As June now ends and seasonality normalises, our bias is to fade the political risk premium

Patrick R Locke (1-212) 834-4254 patrick.r.locke@jpmchase.com J.P. Morgan Securities LLC James Nelligan (44-20) 3493-6829 james.nelligan@jpmorgan.com

in CHF over the short term. In our view this could see EUR/ CHF test 0.97 in the first half of July. Beyond that things get harder for the pair. The June PMI data signalled a set back for Eurozone growth, and it will be key to see whether that repeats in July. EUR/CHF is highly sensitive to Eurozone growth as shown in Figure 31. The SNB also isn’t as dovish as it once was. Jordan clearly express discomfort with the level of the currency at the end of May, and even though he appeared to backtrack on that somewhat at the June policy meeting press conference, the market has a much better idea now of where the line in the sand has been drawn. Additionally, tariff risks are a clear bullish risk for CHF in H2-24. Its our view that once we get to August, it will be difficult for markets to pull their focus away from the probabilities around tariff risk. It will therefore become increasingly difficult for CHF to weaken and indeed there may be good opportunities to go long the currency. Finally, at least as important a risk is that of global yields and the impact on CHF rate spreads. Should the Fed be able to cut more aggressively, and USD/ CHF rate spreads compress, that would be a main driver of any CHF appreciation. For now we think the European political risk premium dominates the aforementioned factors, but then as markets approach the end of July, traditional drivers will re-assert.

## 9. Will DM housing still matter for FX?

Yes. Housing market sensitivity may provide some relief for early-cutter FX, or pressure high holders on a relative basis. In the most intense periods of declines, housing market weakness (and associated growth implications) proved to be a differentiator for FX returns (Figure 33). As G10 central banks cut in a differentiated manner, lower yields can provide relief where currency weakness was associated with rapid hike pass-through to housing market and growth, such as SEK.

As also outlined in our year-ahead outlook, SEK has been our preferred recovery candidate on this basis, as it benefits from a double whammy of housing/growth relief as domestic yields fall, as well as pressure off carry funders as global yields decline. EUR/SEK mispricing has tended to rise in times of housing market declines (Key Currency Views) given the adverse impact of higher yields on the highly indebted economy. NOK and AUD stand out with a high hold relative to their neighbors; hike pass-through is nearcomplete there and debt service ratios have increased markedly. For both NOK and AUD, the economies benefit from commodity sector support as well, making the housing market less significant for FX than in SEK. For AUD, a renewed cooling in the housing market appears likely as rate cuts do not arrive at the schedule households expected earlier in 2024. This may feed back into lower rate hike expectations even if it does not weaken the economy directly. NZD is likely to see some growth relief from cuts but housing is less likely to participate given the ongoing tightening in macroprudential rules which render credit growth and house prices less

Patrick R Locke (1-212) 834-4254 patrick.r.locke@jpmchase.com J.P. Morgan Securities LLC James Nelligan (44-20) 3493-6829 james.nelligan@jpmorgan.com

responsive. In our year-ahead outlook, we expected relief for the likes of SEK but noted the currencies with slower rate pass-through (via a lower share of floating rate mortgages) may be most vulnerable in 2024 as mortgages reset, identifying GBP and CAD.

## 10. Will external balances become a driver for FX again?

Only narrowly. External balances did not drive FX returns again in 1H24; returns have in fact been negatively correlated with surpluses given this is the flipside of carry performance. However, external balances can become a more dominant driver if the prospects of vol/growth shocks intensify; in such episodes, this differentiates performance among the high yielders. Over the period of carry drawdown after the latest shock, the current account balance systematic strategy slightly pared its losses as carry baskets suffered, as a consequence of their negative correlation (Figure 36).

For what it’s worth, current account balances of countries with the largest deficits have improved in G10 and are still healthy for EM, and thus do not present systemic risks but warrant consideration on an RV basis. For instance, NZD’s yield is insufficient reward for the highest current account deficit globally. AUD on the other hand, is projected to have a similar policy rate by year-end as the RBNZ cuts. The basic balance for Japan is near-negative and real yields are the most negative globally, hence pressure is on JPY to weaken without a material change in the yield differential vs US. Among the high yielders, BRL, COP and MXN benefit from the strongest current account support.

## Next three weeks: US payrolls & CPI, global PMIs & CPIs, ECB, RBNZ

Over the next three weeks we will receive important inputs for the global FX market narrative. RBNZ (10<sup>th</sup>; JPM: on hold), and ECB (18<sup>th</sup>; JPM: hold) will be the only G10 central banks to meet alongside a string of EM central banks: Poland (3<sup>rd</sup>; JPM: on hold), Romania (5<sup>th</sup>; JPM: -25bp), Israel (8<sup>th</sup>; JPM: on hold), South Korea (11<sup>th</sup>; JPM: on hold), Malaysia (11<sup>th</sup>; JPM: on hold), Peru (12<sup>th</sup>; JPM: -25bp), South Africa (11<sup>th</sup>; JPM: on hold). CPI data will be released in the US (11<sup>th</sup>), Euro area HICP (2<sup>nd</sup>; Germany 1<sup>st</sup>), Switzerland (4<sup>th</sup>), China (10<sup>th</sup>), Norway (10<sup>th</sup>), Sweden (12<sup>th</sup>), New Zealand (16<sup>th</sup>), UK (18<sup>th</sup>), Japan (19<sup>th</sup>Indonesia (1<sup>st</sup>), Türkiye (3<sup>rd</sup>) and Brazil (10<sup>th</sup>) . On the growth front, global PMIs will include China (30<sup>th</sup> & 1<sup>st</sup>), US ISM (manufacturing 1<sup>st</sup> & services 3<sup>rd</sup>), Sweden, Norway, Switzerland, South Africa, Brazil, Mexico (1<sup>st</sup>). US labour market data also comes out throughout next week: JOLTS (2<sup>nd</sup>), ADP (3<sup>rd</sup>), payrolls (5<sup>th</sup>), while Canada’s does on 5<sup>th</sup>. UK labour market data will be released alongside the CPI on 18<sup>th</sup> July, while China growth data (fixed investment, GDP, IP and retail sales) will be published on 15<sup>th</sup> July.

## Top FX Macro Trades

This section highlights strategic trades for the substantive macro issues that we expect to play out at least through second half of 2024.

Summary on main themes: We stay long USD in the portfolio vs lower-yielding currencies like EUR & CNH, which should outperform given a Fed that is high-for-long and bullish risks from the elections. Policy divergence is still tradable but needs to be more discriminate; stay long AUD/ NZD & NOK/SEK. NOK also to get a boost if yields continue to normalize and boost global growth in the process. The case against JPY mean-reversion remains compelling as G10 carry can remain resilient on wide yield differentials.

Look to scale up USD longs where US election impacts are clearest, but with expressions where premiums are not punitive. Retain USD/CNH topside bias by holding our existing call spread and adding a 9m USD/CNH digi risk reversal (K = 6.95 / 7.35), which features attractive skew. This is a cost-effective structure particularly given the significant premium priced into forward vols, as described in the Outlook. EUR/USD is currently slightly depressed vs FV from French election risk premium; hold EUR/USD 1.03 digi (Aug expiry) but with bias to restructure/reinitiate downside on any post-election relief rallies rally. EUR downside offers a compelling mix of 1) renewed questions on the local macro (EU PMIs), with 2) potential for direct trade conflict, 3) non-prohibitive carry and 4) a (relatively) benign vol surface compared to CNH & MXN.

No longer at peak US exceptionalism, but the dollar still benefits from US-derived supports. US exceptionalism may be moderating but still pieces remain intact, including the high-for-long Fed, US equity outperformance, stronger US productivity, and bullish risks stemming from the upcoming US election. And while the US-RoW growth gap had been narrowing from both sides in recent weeks, the latest EU PMIs have poured some cold water on the theme of growth rotating away from the US. We keep a tactical trading approach as in 1H, and would look to add long-USD exposure, particularly if USD richness on short-term measures fades.

Be more surgical in policy r.v. trades. Policy r.v. proved profitable in G10 broadly in 1H (USD, NOK, AUD, CAD, JPY). Policy paths can remain distinct, but 2H does point to more convergence than divergence vs 1H (eg Fed easing). That suggests narrower - but plausible - opportunities to trade policy r.v. Hold long AUD/NZD given risks of RBA hikes and RBNZ dovish capitulation in August). Long NOK/SEK with Norges Bank raising its rates profile (JPM Dec) vs Riksbank pointing to 2-3 more eases

this year.

You’re saying there’s still a chance? Lower yields boost growth, and cheap/rate-sensitive G10 FX by extension. The G10 high-beta bloc is already well-priced for a recession, which suggests that these are attractive candidates to go long should the global soft landing play out. We prefer currencies that have high gearing to global rates and are still cheap - and preferably with domestic kickers. NOK, with the hawkish Norges Bank, checks that box (short EUR/NOK; short EUR/SEK also viable).

G10 carry still viable on lesser overall narrowing in yield dispersion; strategic JPY weakness intact but requires nimble management, compared to CHF where tactical weakness runs into tariff risks. DM yield differentials should stay wide so long as inflation remains sticky and keeps the high-yielders cautious on cutting rates. Even multiple BoJ hikes this year doesn’t materially erode negative domestic real rates, leaving material gaps vs RoW and incentive for ongoing capital outflows. But we have taken profit on long AUD/JPY, and are cognizant of the MoF’s sensitivities around dislocations from FV and spikes in volatility. Stay short JPY, but cognizant of intervention risk - and being opportunistic if and when it does occur. CHF can weaken in early July on European political risk reduction and seasonality normalisation, but beyond that things get tricky as tariff risks come into view.

## Emerging Markets FX

We remain MW overall as EM FX will likely be held back in 2H24 by the looming US elections, while macro uncertainties remain unresolved. Within an overall MW stance, we favour idiosyncratic carry stories and currencies with attractive valuations.

EMEA EM: We remain OW EMEA EM FX via OW TRY and RON vs. UW CZK. We position for a potential regional cyclical uplift via 2-month 1x1 389/381 EURHUF put spreads (see here) and have a medium-term 3.40 USDILS digital put.

LatAm: LatAm high yielders continue to hurt, with the spike in volatility decreasing the appeal of the carry profile in the region. The more challenging backdrop has led us to reduce risk in LatAm, particularly as we move closer to the US election. We are now MW BRL, PEN and CLP, UW COP and OW MXN and UYU. This still keeps us OW LatAm FX in the model portfolio but with a smaller bias.

EM Asia:Stay UW (via THB and CNH) in the GBI-EM portfolio but initiate INR at OW. Stay engaged with carry but remain defensive. Limiting USD length till valuations and positioning clean up, but bias for USDAsia higher into the US elections. Stay short CNHINR and TWDPHP, long SGD vs basket and short CNH (vs AUD, SGD basket and via options).

## EM FX Outlook for 2H24: Keep less calm, but carry (is still) on

Keep MW FX overall while hiding out in idiosyncratic carry. Trading a strong global macro trend for EM FX does not look like a great risk/reward strategy to us at the current juncture. First, there still remains a degree of ‘observational equivalence’ in the US and global data: on the one hand there is still a tight US labor market which can generate sticky inflation, with few signs of corporate or household imbalances, so rates may stay high-for-long. On the other hand, growth looks to be slowing with our economists forecasting this to continue in 2H24 and the cumulative impact of rate hikes may still be lagging through the economy. EM assets have become tired of trading these macro variations, as each scenario has looked plausible over the last two years. The bar for further Fed hikes is currently high but the rates landscape may again change post-US election. This brings us to the second issue for EM markets, which is a US election cycle in which some of the policy proposals may significantly impact specific EM countries (see section later and also 2024 US Election Watch) . Given a lack of risk-premia in EM assets ahead of this, there is an element of complacency. On the positive side of the ledger, we still think EM has good idiosyn-

cratic carry. Harvesting this idiosyncratic carry while remaining hedged against market beta is how we are currently recommending to position within EM.

## EMEA EM: Conviction on TRY; mixed expressions elsewhere

We head into the second half of the year OW EMEA EM FX mainly via the idiosyncratic Turkiye story. The EMEA EM region is fundamentally well set, in our view: moderate growth recovery is underway, inflation is contained, real yields are high in historical ranges and current accounts have recovered. However, the competition from US yields remains fierce, US election risks loom and several idiosyncratic risks linger (coalition stability in SA, geopolitics in Israel) and several currencies screen expensive and/or overpositioned. We thus retain a bullish portfolio but mainly via TRY and we hold more mixed FX expressions elsewhere.

Specifically, we OW TRY and RON vs. UW CZK. We replicate these positions also in outright trades with a long in EURCZK and short USDTRY April 2025 forwards. We position for a potential European regional cyclical uplift via 2-month 1x1 389/381 EURHUF put spreads (see here) and have a medium-term 3.40 USDILS digital put. The table below provides further details.

TRY: Bullish lira is currently our highest conviction view in the region. We see monetary conditions as tight with now likely substantially positive real yields in the period ahead (Figure 47) and credit growth contained. In addition to tight monetary policy, fiscal policy is now turning tight with about -2% GDP projected annualized change in the fiscal deficit between H1 2024 and H2. The preliminary trade balance for May was in seasonally adjusted terms best since October 2021 and consistent with about -\$1bn seasonally adjusted current account deficit. We believe tight monetary and fiscal conditions can improve the BoP position further. While positioning in FX appears extended, given the macro fundamentals and historical volumes of trading, we do not feel it is particularly problematic. We recommend an OW stance and also short USDTRY 12m fwd (see here).

ZAR: We have now returned to our pre-election constructive bias on ZAR but wait for better entry levels. We see a potential for an inflection point in SA’s fundamentals. Specifically, improvements in energy availability and prospects for lower logistical bottlenecks should lift SA’s potential growth while we also expect stronger efforts at fiscal consolidation and potentially lower SARB inflation target. The GNU arrangement can increase the confidence on these drivers, although coalition stability and unity remains uncertain. Besides fundamentals, there are other factors that are in ZAR’s favor: ZAR has gained in carry rankings and it is an under-owned currency compared to other carry plays while locals have likely reached peak foreign asset allocations. However, the market has been quick to re-price following the elections and we currently view the currency as expensive vs. short term drivers and wait for better entry levels.

CZK: Long EUR/CZK and UW in the GBI-EM Model Portfolio. CNB cut 50bps at its last meeting and we believe the terminal rate for the central bank is still underpriced. CZK is also expensive in long-term valuations, which we believe has contributed to stagnating FDI inflows, underperforming the region.

We are not inclined to be short Zloty, given some chances of a cyclical regional uplift and NBP hawkishness, but we see heightened risks for PLN with Zloty likely the EMEA EM US election hedge of choice.

Our OW in RON serves primarily as a convenient construct to hedge EURUSD risk in our portfolio.

## Latin America FX: Reducing risk in Latam amid a more challenging backdrop

Latam high-yielders are still hurting. A sharp increase in political noise has shattered BRL and MXN markets, with positioning likely playing a relevant role in exacerbating the magnitude of the recent moves,as both currencies have been among the “consensus longs” for the past couple of years. COP has not been immune to the shock wave, as the fisca picture continues to deteriorate in a context of rate cuts.

The “buy the dip” mentality that had capped periods of weakness last year has been more muted through the latest wave of depreciation, particularly for BRL, leading to more ample and persistent losses. Bouts of volatility are embedded in the nature of carry trades, and, as such, BRL and MXN are no strangers to them. In the most recent episode, however, BRL’s inability to find relief amid benign global data (e.g., downside surprise in US CPI) and a more hawkish central bank that unanimously voted to keep rates on hold at 10.5% might be signaling a change in the mindset of inves-

tors and raises questions around the durability of BRL as a carry vehicle.

While sentiment around Brazilian assets is still depressed, we think there is reasonable asymmetry for BRL put spreads at current levels. The unwind of the bullish BRL trade has not been driven by a sharp deterioration of the fundamental backdrop for Brazil but by a strong worsening of the sentiment, which is difficult to calibrate ahead (see our recent analysis here) and has led us to move MW both Brazil FX and local rates in the model portfolio (from OW). Still, much better valuations and cleaner positioning (our JPM BRL Positioning indicator is already in neutral territory <JPRABRLP Index>) leave space for relief in the coming weeks, and we positioned for that possibility via a 24-Sep 1x2 USDBRL put spread (5.30/5.05 with mids at 139 vs 32, for a net premium of 75bps; FX spot ref: 5.3877).

Meanwhile, MXN has been recovering slowly from the post-election shock as the communication of the new government maintains a market friendly tone. Stay OW. With the information available so far, we are keeping our view that Mexico’s strong fundamental tailwinds will not be derailed by the incoming administration, and we find current valuations attractive for medium- and long-term investors. The confirmation of Rogelio Ramirez de la O as Minister of Finance alongside other well-regarded individuals in the cabinet (Ebrard in the Ministry of Economy and De la Fuente as Minister of Foreign Affairs) are positive signals. The reiterated messages of fiscal prudence and central bank autonomy also suggest that financial market signals are being heeded.

Lingering political uncertainty, however, raises the bar for Banxico to deliver more cuts than want the market s already pricing. The market is pricing a rate of \~10.55% for year-end from the current level of 11%. We think that, despite a relatively benign inflation trend, increased political noise and heightened volatility in local markets will continue to limit the market’s ability to price in more easing in the near term, particularly given the narrowing window until the US election.

Hedging against the potential for more weakness: Stay UW COP (fom MW) in the model portfolio. It is difficult to predict contagion, particularly in the absence of a US rates or commodity price shock, but the COP’s carry looks unlikely to protect the currency from another leg in the the sell-off, especially as fiscal rule discussions will intensify in 2H and the easing cycle will potentially accelerate. The Colombian peso has traded strongly throughout most of the year, unabated by fiscal noise, with high carry discouraging shorts. A number of negative fiscal developments have taken place, but none has

been able to materially weaken the currency. COP has thus lagged the moves in peers and seems more vulnerable to a correction at current levels, especially with Banrep having delivered 150bp of cutting and could soon be accelerating its easing cycle, as inflation behaves in line with its base case. We keep an outright long COLTES 2026 as there is room for more cuts to be priced-in.

In Chile, maintain our tactical long USD/CLP. BCCh cut 25bp to 5.75%, in line with consensus, but said the bulk of the easing for this year was likely done. This is a material shift for a central bank that earlier in the year was discussing reaching neutral levels (3.50-4.50% as per BCCh) in 2H as soon as possible. Our economist now sees only one further 25bp cut in 2024 (to 5.50%), in line with the market. The change in BCCh’s inflation forecasts suggests an outlook of more persistent higher prices, with December 2024 headline CPI expected to end above the target range (4.2%). As such, a terminal policy rate slightly above 5% for next year seems unlikely, and we enter 2y payers. Meanwhile, BTP bonds continue to struggle amid underwhelming auctions and book building offers, which further supports our UW rates. A more hawkish central bank, however, is unlikely to drive CLP strength given real rates could continue to deteriorate, while the surge in copper has been correcting, leading to foreign investors potentially rebuilding their CLP shorts.

In Peru, we are MW PEN (from OW) in the model portfolio. As copper prices have corrected downward following a strong rally, we think PEN has now more limited room to appreciate on the back of strong terms of trade. USD inflows on the back of the pension fund withdrawal bill might be behind the currency’s outperformance in Latam but seem unlikely to drive more strength, especially given a fiscal outlook that has been deteriorating on the margin and low interest rate differentials. In rates, we remain MW in the model portfolio, with Soberanos sales from pension funds an ongoing risk.

From here, the US election will be the risk to watch. Given the narrowing window until the US election, risks become more prominent and we can see a choppy summer without a strong trend. Before 2016, US elections were generally not significant for EM markets, with assets usually performing somewhat better after elections as uncertainty was removed. 2016 was a departure from that as the Trump victory led to a significant EM asset sell-off, with countries such as Mexico and China seeing particularly large moves given specific policies that impacted their trade relationships with the US. The fact that there may be less of an election surprise or policy novelty this time, and the better state of EM fundamentals and technicals, should help mitigate the extent of moves versus those seen post-2016. But with little clear election risk

Global FX Strategy FX Mid-Year Outlook 28 June 2024

premia so far priced into EM assets, we would again expect a reaction if the election result brings in a set of policies that impact EM markets.

The risks around the November US election are likely skewed negatively at this point, and we will look to adjust Latam risk for the election scenarios as we get closer to this event. So far, none of the candidates has adopted a clear narrative that would imply the need for major changes in the commercial or political relationship with Mexico, which is a welcome outcome for local assets, but it is still early in the campaign. We are keeping our MXN hedge via -3M /+6M digital calendar spread (sell 22-Aug (3M) USD/MXN 18.50 digital call to finance long 20-Nov (6M) 18.50 digital call (spot ref 16.63)).

## EM Asia FX: Staying underweight as carry remains a key, but so is (counter) cyclicality

A difficult first half for EM Asia: Low yielding EM Asia saw broad FX weakness with the re-pricing of US rates, offsetting a nascent export recovery in some parts of the manufacturing sector. Energy importers were the major underperformers, given the 12% rally in crude YTD, with the THB, TWD, KRW and PHP leading the way lower. IDR also put in major underperformance, with much of this coming in the 2Q as the prospect of a less fiscally disciplined government drove significant outflows from IndoGBs. INR was the top performing currency across the whole FX complex in 1H, as flat is the new up and carry adding to elevated risk adjusted gains. Despite significant bearish interest and ongoing grown concerns, the CNH was a relative outperformer, in Asia, sending the RMB Index +2.6% higher. Valuations have begun to look stretched for USDAsia longs, however, with the cluster trading +1.7z above rate differential implied fair value and USD length against low yielders - against JPY and CHF in CFTC CoT report - looking stretched. For this reason, we remain USD neutral for now but look to re-engage given headwinds for Asi FX in 2H.

Carry remains a key, but so is (counter) cyclicality: Despite carry unwinds in other parts of EM, Asian carry trades continue to offer cleaner positioning, attractive carry to vol ratios, and limited drawdowns due to two-sided central bank intervention. Relative fundamentals continue to favour domestically-oriented high yielders funded with trade reliant low yielders, which coincidentally also offer idea cyclical orientation. Moving into 2H, we continue to stay defensively positioned in carry trades, favouring domestically oriented high yielders over trade reliant low yielders. We start the half with shorts in CNHINR and TWDPHP as dividend seasons likely weigh on CNH and TWD while easing political uncertainty and disruption to remittances should support INR and PHP, respectively. We retain Underweights in CNH and THB on the GBI-EM Portfolio and initiate INR at OW.

Elections to be a major risk for EM Asia FX in 2H. The beta-centrepiece of Asian FX dynamics in H2 is likely to be the US election / tariff related pressures on CNY FX, which is likely to compound ongoing BoP pressures arising from adverse US-Asia yield differentials. During the 2018/’19 trade war, every 1% tariff rate was associated with roughly a 0.7% rise in \$CNY. Based on the same metric, an average tariff rate of 60% that has been mooted by the Trump campaign would mathematically imply a tariff neutral level close to 9 for \$CNY, though there are good reasons to think why this is an overestimate this time given the reduced shock value of tariffs overall, lower US-China bilateral trade shares, and the much reduced exchange rate elasticity of Chinese exports. Nonetheless, with little trace of geopolitical risk premium in \$CNY, the RMB is likely to remain under pressure over most of H2. Given the NEER targeting nature of FX policy regimes in EM Asia, significant RMB weakness is also likely to spillover into regional currencies, particularly amongst the low
