yielding trade reliant cluster of small open economies (i.e. TW, KR, SG, TH, MY). For investors, the challenge will be to navigate the cross-currents between this top-down drag on the Asia FX bloc and idiosyncratic positive developments in certain pockets (India GBI EM inclusion, seasonal late Q3/ early Q4 tech lift in N. Asia, potential WGBI inclusion for KTBs in Q3, late Q4 tourism revival in Thailand, remittance revival in PH, etc.). On the whole, our relative value preference within the region remains for higher-yielding currencies in S.Asia’s relatively closed economies over those in the smaller, more trade-exposed economies of N.Asia.

Figure 62: USD/CNY and the tariff-offsetting level
![](images/fc1b3703469d15908b48f50285ba5a8dce646209d6fc35466eb10c9a9ddde85c.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan

Tariff wars to broaden, collateral damage is due: In terms of direct exposure to US tariffs, we believe countries with a large share in US imports and large gains from rebalancing away from China’s imports since 2017 are most at risk (Figure 63). Several tiers of tariff priority emerge from this comparison. The highest priority includes the European Union, Mexico and Vietnam, but the meaty middle tier of focus consists of EM Asia’s small open trade reliant economies, which may collectively also be seen as the highest priority with a total trade surplus of \$340bn vs the US. Combined with high betas to the RMB in the region, EM Asian FX are likely to see several additional headwinds in 2H from (geo)political cross currents.

Figure 63: US Import share vs bilateral trade deficit by economy Countries not shown have lost US import share since 2017
![](images/103253e8ae763179d912a66770170bf3d7388acdc37d8f3872e21d8d03d49302.jpg)
Source: IMF DOTS, J.P. Morgan estimates.

Spillovers to EM Asia are NEER(s). Given the NEER targeting nature of FX policy regimes in the EM Asia region, significant RMB weakness is also likely to spill over into regional currencies, particularly amongst the low-yielding trade reliant cluster of small open economies (ie TW, KR, SG, TH, MY). Considering the weights of China in the NEER baskets of each currency, exposures range from 22 to 35%. Coupling this with the empirical regression betas to CNH, the batting order of exposures generally hits KRW the hardest, followed by THB, TWD, MYR and SGD. The high yielding cluster of INR, IDR and PHP are generally more insulated from RMB weakness, but betas are still positive and non-zero. The challenge for EM Asia will be the secondary effects, given Asia’s low yielders also represent a significant chunk of NEER baskets everywhere. Including Asia low yielders, NEER basket exposures jump into the 40-50% range, which risks another round of competitive devaluation pressure, especially if outflow pressures persist. Combined with the risk of tariffs on a broader set of countries, especially ‘China plus one’ beneficaries, the pressure on EM Asia FX is likely to remain.

![](images/f89b6a9b19ba0df264988fbe7166f0792bfd0b1b7ca7e0f05dc00434c083c8a0.jpg)

![](images/e4d8adb9c5f01957726734bda02e932a685c19aa32000740f9a3d32c7e42310d.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan estimates.

Context may offer some reprieve: While the 2018 tariff war serves as a useful model for how FX performance may evolve under the next administrations’s foreign policy, macroeconomic context is important to consider. Going into 2018, Asian economies were getting inflows as the USD had softened materially. A red sweep, expansionary US fiscal policy and broad a export cycle uplift had supported EM Asia FX amid the Fed’s (gradual) rate hiking cycle. Going into 2025, Asian economies have been struggling with outflows after a 3y bull market in the USD, a narrow to soft trade outlook, and (potentially) an unwind of an aggressive Fed tightening cycles. Markets have held a bearish outlook on EM Asia FX for several years now, and incremental negativity, especially around China, may have less of an impact due to cleaner or even negative positioning. Finally, financial conditions via growth spillovers are also key, with market concerns deteriorating rapidly from 2Q18 as concerns around a tariff-driven recession emerged. In that context, its notable that the RMB index a significant casualty early on, falling from 98 to 92 as to compensate for tariffs, but broader growth concerns dominated in 4Q18, taking oil lower together with more cyclically exposed EM FX, resulting in EM Asia outperformance through the end of the year.

Figure 66: FX Performance in 2018 Performance vs USD from Apr to Sep vs Sep to Dec
![](images/ced77583271a4e32275fa177e0797a8a6dfa6dda569e468c6726cb1b7adcd4d5.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan estimates.

India remains the top pick in Asia but vulnerabilities rise in the 2H: We initiate INR at OW in the GBI-EM portfolio and remain short CNHINR in outright trades as India remains the place to be in EM Asia FX. Carry continues to look attractive to us despite fundamental improvements in the current account, allowing the RBI to deliver on its FX stability goals and further improve risk-adjusted returns. The rupee’s low cyclical beta makes it a uniquely counter-cyclical play, particularly useful in times of defensive biases in a pro-cyclical EM portfolio. Valuations are not broadly prohibitive as yet, but sensitivity to the RMB remains, which could create vulnerability in 4Q, closer to the US elections. For now, services continue to support the current account and portfolio flows should be sufficient to plug the gap, owing to index inclusion and foreign equity investors’ underweights. FX Investors’ positioning has likely cleaned up post elections, suggesting that outflow pressures will be manageable for the RBI despite a seasonal weakening in the current account due to the new fiscal year. Our preferred expression remains INR longs vs CNH, which we think offer the most attractive exante carry to vol ratio in the FX complex, while often delivering even more ex-post (see here).

Figure 67: Top FX Carry trades – Cyclical beta vs Carry to Vol ratio 3m ex-ante carry to realized vol, 1y rolling beta to SPX, BCOM Indices
![](images/c1f36cdb67dd87f16e6058634a9feffeffe3d06019adfdc241f1de0ce58884f4.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan estimates, Bloomberg Finance L.P.

Figure 68: 3m INR NDF Implied yield vs CA, %GDP Current vs 10y mean
![](images/400de45f08ab66752772ed27dd03820d7364ca8434f78b37c4ec96ca30b19bb3.jpg)
Source: Reserve Bank of India, J.P. Morgan estimates, Bloomberg Finance L.P.

Stay long SGD for carry as policy stays tighter for longer.The geopolitically motivated onshoring of services jobs has driven an imbalance in the labour market, adding immigration and in turn spurring non-core components of inflation via demand for housing and private transport. The vacancies to unemployment ratio has cooled from its 2.5x peak but remains significantly elevated at 1.7x, materially higher than the long-term average of 1.0x, and the same metric in the US which remains 1.2x. The labour market has shown continued signs of cooling with sluggish global demand driving a retrenching of manufacturing labour amid still firm hiring from the services sector. Policy makers have broadened their tool kit this cycle, leaning on various non-monetary policy tools like migration, administrative and fiscal support, while also tweaking both FX and rates levers on the monetary front. These other measures are running into constraints, however, which likely leaves monetary policy the primary tool to address inflationary pressures. MAS’ own domestic liquidity indicator suggests a mean reversion of approach, after using both FX and rates to tighten liquidity aggressively in 2022. We continue to think FX policy should be the primary tool for the small open economy, particularly in consideration of its secondary channels to deal with inflation by influencing demand, particularly if the global manufacturing cycle accelerates. We remain long SGD (vs an equally weighted basket of EUR, CNH, JPY, TWD and THB) as carry and drift remains attractive on these dynamics.

Figure 69: MAS Domestic liquidity indicator vs Core inflation Contribution from FX, rates vs Core CPI y/y
![](images/9a21ce17d99ba2162925c85f0b49a02237d9352a13f1a287aa145cfd0e0861d4.jpg)
Source: Monetary Authority of Singapore, , J.P. Morgan estimates.

We turn more neutral on KRW, with headwinds potentially flipping to tailwinds.The won has been one of the worst performers in Asia, having been hit hard by tightening financial conditions, higher energy prices, and seasonal weakness from energy imports and dividend outflows. Demographics continue to motivate significant outflows, while rate differentials weigh on FX conversion and FX-impactful portfolio inflows. Our outlook for the 2H is cautiously optimistic, however, with the handset release cycle typically driving a seasonal uptick in the electronics exports, feeding through into greater FX conversion later. Exports have mattered less for KRW this year, with investment outflows serving to recycle the improved surplus as demographics leaves domestic investment prospects weak. The 3Q typically sees less outflows, however, which may momentarily place greater importance on exporter conversion. The potential inclusion of KTBs into the WGBI index could be a key game changer for Korea’s balance of payments, with inflows likely to be worth double the current account. The key risk is a re-acceleration of US exceptionalism that also drives the USD stronger, particularly into the US elections where trade policy is likely to emerge as a key channel of transmission for Asia FX.

## FX Derivatives

## FX vol bumpy but still capped in H2

• FX vol remained largely sideways in H1, and is entering H2 with a familiar undervalued feel. We look for US election and periodic carry stress related spot noise to animate local gamma in coming months, but for vols to remain contained overall amid a broadly supportive global growth environment and lower rates.

The core vol surface stance is to favor gamma over convexity; buy GBP/USD 1M ATM straddles vs. 10D strangles, equal notionals.

US elections pricing is tracking previous cycles and with close to 4M mark to the election G10 pricing could have 5-6vol upside. Buy -4M / +8M SEK or HUF vol calendars and USD/CNH 9M digital risk reversals.

● Carry unwind and snap French elections pushed pockets of risk premia sharply higher. Harvest the residual premium in USD/MXN via 1\*2 ratio put spreads. Buy -1M / +2M EUR/USD downside one-touch calendars and sell EUR correlations in 2M EUR/GBP-EUR/USD or EUR/GBP-EUR/CAD.

Macro uncertainty calls for long-end defensive vol RVs that are also supported by our mean-reversion model: buy HUF vs EUR and buy AUD vs SEK (1y vol swaps); Fade French-election richness in EUR risk reversal with a defensive skew RV trade: buy a 1Y 25D risk-reversal d-h in NZD, sell in EUR.

Vol pricing in the JPY-complex favors the constructs: a) buy 150 strike put, sell 155 strike put in +1.5/-1 options notionals; b) buy USD/JPY vs. CHF/JPY as a defensive vol spread earning positive vol carry; c) play a lower JPY via USD/JPY, USD/NOK lower dual-digi.

We consider hedges for two what-if scenarios: (a) if the DXY weakens substantially — own deep OTM USD put digitals vs. high carry EM FX; and (b) if equities crack — inverse 10D/25D ratio put spreads in CHF and JPY crosses (AUD/CHF and GBP/JPY), or [SX5E lower, EUR/CHF lower] dual digitals.

## H2 FX vol view: Bumpy, but still broadly capped

1H24 report card — FX vol went nowhere fast. Outside of the late Q2 washout in EM FX carry, FX volatility was by and large out of commission through H1 as we had generally anticipated coming into the year (FX Vol: Not going anywhere fast). The ebbing of rate volatility contributed meaningfully to lackluster FX vols, as the late’23 optimism around muscular rate cuts gave way to the reality of policy inaction for the most part in the face of sticky inflation. Alpha generation via FX options rested primarily on creative ways of earning carry: selling USD puts in low-yielding, especially US election exposed currencies worked well (CNY, TWD, JPY), as did calendar spreads of HY EM FX calls that rolled positively over time. A timely pivot towards owning CHF-cross protection in Q2 after H1’s stellar run of CHF-funded G10 carry worked out well through bouts of LatAm FX deleveraging, but we were too early to consider even selective hedges against EM carry earlier in the year; indeed, the under-performance of EM risk-reversals despite distinctly unimpressive spot performance (e.g. BRL) was a revelation.

Figure 72: FX vols screen cheap when benchmarked to macro growth and rate vol
![](images/9676100c0173b9410cc1f444f78f15da72081bb3e1299eabe0a5207c80548b58.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P., J.P.Morgan

Vols are entering 2H24 with a familiar undervalued feel. VXY Global screens1.2 - 1.5 % pts. contemporaneously low when benchmarked to business cycle correlates such as PMIs, or more tellingly, relative to rates vol (Figure 72). In the normal course of events, it would have been difficult to get excited about this vol cheapness at a time when Global PMIs are running above 50 and PMI strength is broadening out across a wider swathe of countries, and when an increasing number of central banks — none more important than the Fed — look likely to embark on rate cutting cycles. This almost mid-cycle flavor to the global macro set-up is hardly the recipe for a risk market meltdown that can motivate a bullish volatility view, and our reticence is amplified by two additional technical considerations:

USD speculative positioning (basis IMMs) remains long despite the recent normalization, which is also corroborated by alternative positioning metrics that we track (link). While the intuitive inverse relationship between USD positions and FX vol (i.e. overly bearish USD positions = excessive currently risk-seeking behavior = future comeuppance in the form of high vol) has loosened post-Covid

Seasonals are not particularly favorable for FX vol through the first half of Q3 before turning more constructive post the summer lull (Figure 73Seasonl rtpicuy fvb FX h Q3, g - m). Anecdotal client conversations also reveal low investor appetite to add option-based macro risk ahead of summer holidays given the murkiness around the US/Fed cycle, mature DXY valuations, paucity of YTD P/L, and the distraction of quadrennial sporting events.

Fed easing v.2024 is not a complete vol killer however. Our conviction that a repeat of 4Q22 and 4Q23 is not on the cards is based on a simple, yet powerful observation that assets are expensive relative to the global cycle (Figure 74With noracef ylsk pmugb w, Fd Q4   - v), which means that there is no cyclical risk premium to be squeezed out from financial markets via disinflationary Fed cuts in a way that might have been possible after the risk parity convulsions of the past two years. Instead, the balance of risks around the Fed cycle is asymmetric to the extent that the FOMC’s best laid easing plans of 9 cuts through 2026 can be upended by stubbornly high inflation requiring another round of 1Q24 style re-pricing of the USD OIS strip. Our best guess is that this is realistically a 2025 rather than a 2H24 risk, but CPI data has stumped forecasters too many times in this cycle for anyone to be truly convicted on timing. Scalping CPI gamma has been a sound vol strategy all year (Figure 75), and is likely to remain so, especially in smaller G7 pairs such as NOK and SEK rather than the majors (Figure 76).

The wildcard — US elections. Nearly every sell-side midyear outlook is likely to flag the pre-US election jostling in markets as the centerpiece of 2H24 price action, and rightfully so given the high stakes around tariffs, geopolitics and US fiscal policy. One could argue that it is in fact somewhat surprising as to why the rising odds of a Trump Presidency in betting markets has not elicited a bigger reaction in fixed income markets already. Perhaps the distance to the event still, the reduced shock value of the Trump manifesto this time around, unknowns around Congressional composition, and the negative carry of Trump hedges have all conspired to keep a lid on vol so far, but this will likely change as election day draws nearer. FX option markets can credibly argue to have been ahead of the curve in pricing in a substantial event risk premium for the day of the election, but the experience of 2016 and 2020 shows that outsized market moves may have to wait for after the elections once the legislative degrees of freedom to execute the President-elect’s platform becomes clearer. Asian currencies in the eye of the tariff / US - China geopolitical storm, as well as European FX that has exposure to Russia/Ukraine geopolitics stand to be bigger vol beneficiaries of US political noise than others. Expect 4Q24 to prove bumpy in this respect.

Vol surface bias into H2: preference for gamma over convexity. Our macro team anticipates the USD to trade firm but not run away higher given the impending Fed easing cycle and the overall direction of travel towards monetary convergence (more central banks easing in H2), but the ride to be bumpy given election noise and periodic challenges for carry trades amidst narrower yield differential buffers. This is fundamentally a recipe for local gamma ownership, but not one that motivates paying up a lot of premium for deep OTM tails, especially as some of the more extreme post-election scenarios that can promote outsized trend moves may have to wait till late Q4 when the composition of the Congress is known with certainty. Happily, going into H2, initial vol surface pricing conditions support a long ATM vs. short surface stance, with Figure 77 showing that RR/ ATM and BF / ATM ratios are now back up to near \~1-yr highs. While the bulk of the convexity premium at present is concentrated in European FX owing to French election event risk, riskies and flies also screen elevated vs. ATMs in some Asian FX (KRW, SGD, JPY), though somewhat oddly not so in other EMs potentially subject to political noise (ZAR, IDR). We open a long 1M straddle vs. short 1M 15D strangle (equal notionals) in GBP/USD as an opening gambit for this class of trades in H2, since GBP spot screens very rich on our STFV regressions, spec positioning buildup in the currency has been substantial and our ML models advocate a long gamma stance.

## Fading late Q2 vintage carry washout and/ or European political risk premium

Carry washout premium harvesting: Recent election surprises caused rapid deterioration of sentiment and kicked off dramatic string of carry unwinds that heavily impacted Latam and prompted a multi sigma reaction in MXN and BRL vol. Normalization is well underway with MXN riskes by now having consolidated the washout pop by 70%, ahead of atm vols which consolidated the spike by only 45% for 1M expiry and further out ATM vol retracement is lagging even more (Figure 78). The residual realized vol has been slowing down further vol consolidation.

Figure 78: Skew normalization has been swift in MXN but ATM vols are lagging the historical trends.
![](images/b3f66e38a405024cbfe4294160d1bca9eb7de195ac24d1025b3019b40711e9d1.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan.

The quickly approaching Nov US Election is shrinking the window for normalization but considering the overall backdrop one type of trades that would leverage it while not taking unnecessary risks is vanilla USD/MXN ratio put spreads.

3M 18.30/17.75 (close to 35d / 19d strikes) 1\*2 ratio @28bps (on mids) comes at 80% discount to an outright vanilla and doubles in value under unchanged market conditions by the 1-mo to expiry mark.

How to sell EUR premium with contained downside exposure? The snap French election announcement pushed EUR and regional currencies vols sharply higher on the back of wide range of possible outcome. Our analyst expects EUR to settle at 1.075 under the baseline scenario of RN winning a relative majority resulting in a cohabitation government, with less likely scenarios seeing it move to 1.060 or 1.085, representing spot moves of 0.9% and 1.4%, respectively.

Figure 80: Current cycle pricing on par with 2022 Presidential. Aggregate EUR breakevens at around 1.4%.
Current French snap election pricing vs 2022 Presidential

Source: J.P. Morgan.

FXO markets are currently pricing the 2 rounds at close to 15vol each (Figure 80Curent cylpig oawh20 Psd. AEUR bkv 14%), corresponding to 1.4% aggregate EUR breakevens, which is largely encapsulating the rates and cash analysts edge scenarios.

The two-rounds voting adds uncertainty due to risks for markets to whipsaw between rounds due to lack of clarity on votes transfer as the field narrows for the 2nd round. It’s notable that this cycle pricing is nearly comparable to pricing of the 2022 French presidential election at just ahead of the vote and about 60% from the peak pricing. Last week we discussed capturing spillover noise and options for hedging the event risk. But with Frexit not on the table and the key issue being the question of fiscal side in a Le Pen majority, market reaction should be contained and and risks skewed toward event vols underperformance, but the whipsaw risks linger and the backdrop may remain particularly fluid between the two rounds.

Given the overall backdrop, EUR downside one-touch calendar spreads strike us as a balanced limited downside premium fading approach. These calendars could be particularly effective in event of a relief-driven EUR pop higher in the aftermath of the second round and are a cheap way of positioning into EUR downside for later on ahead of the July PMIs and the ECB (a skip expected before a potential Sep cut), and as focus turns to the US election. Static carry metrics (Figure 81) ranks -1M/+2M EUR/USD one-touch put calendar spreads as one of the best. The structure effectively buys 2M EUR downside 1.05 digital for 11.5%, i.e. at >70% discount. EUR has been at the 1.05 barrier last in Oct 2023. The max loss is the premium paid vs max payout / cost 9X.

Same strike on both legs, set so that 2M leg strikes corresponds to 50% TV. In practice that means for e.g. EUR/USD that barrier is at 1.054 for spot at 1.0712, or 1.8% OTM. EUR was last at 1.054 in Oct 2023. A more conservative configuration e.g. with EUR/USD strike at 1.049 (i.e. 40% TV) would correspond to a static carry of 16%.

![](images/35bb81372ecb9a95c981bf826e1a18997bdc9d54df5ba0470779e3eef69ad031.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan.

Selling EUR correlations tends to be a profitable strategy (Figure 82) and due to French elections EUR corrs are currently multi-sigma elevated even after some consolidation since the initial spike following the surprise announcment. EUR/GBP-EUR/USD 2M corr at 33% (mids at 37,+2.6 sigma on 2-y window, levels last seen ahead of 2022 Presidential election). Back then the corrs held firm, collapsing toward 20% only after the 2<sup>nd</sup> round. Realized corr was below 10% at the start of the month. Liquidity permitting EUR/GBP-EUR/CAD setup is even better with current level 47% and implied realized spread close to 25corr pts. Consider selling 2M EUR/GBP - EUR/CAD correlation swap at 43.

## US election lightly priced in EUR and JPY

Event pricing tracking prior cycles and is consistent with projections/polling: As this year is shaping up to be one of election surprises, market participants are increasingly fixated on event risk. Still, so far FX options markets US election pricing has been taking the cue from the previous cycles, the 2020 in particular. By our estimates, the current aggregate pricing is now close to fair, having declined from last month's peak and positioned between betting market odds and swing state polling spreads.

Figure 84: At near the fair value, FXO event risk pricing middling between polling spreads and predictions (betting markets).
![](images/ab6c64e61f91deeaf83821ca9ca38a1ddf27ef815e50dadd4c087569ea0944e5.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan, RealClear, PredictIt, Bloomberg Finance L.P.

Following a period of synchronized increases in both pricing and betting markets/polls that were driven by heightened attention and election-focused headlines, future premiums are expected to be influenced by scenario risks and their associated probabilities. Tighter polls and increased uncertainty are likely to command higher event risk pricing, even in the absence of a shock surprise. The 1.2% USD TWI breakevens look consistent with the spot market premium assessment.

## Pricing details :

With just under four months until the November 5 US Election Day, and the election debates just ahead, there is a heightened focus on potential election surprises, influenced by recent events in Mexico, South Africa, and India. It is not surprising then that CNH volatility is already at prior cycle highs, with a 7-8 point spread compared to the corresponding levels in 2020 and when Wed and Thursday post-event premium are both accounted for the total corresponds to \~1.5% in the USD/CNH breakevens.

Regional spillover effects have led to elevated event volatilities for KRW and TWD, with the three leading the way in the relative rankings (Figure 86). The elevated event vols and steep rolldown discourage outright election ownership. The KRW to CNH spot beta is favorable at close to 1:1 although the relationship is somewhat casual. KRW may be more susceptible to selling pressure, whereas for CNH it is PBoC that tends to have the final say.

Additionally, the elevated KRW skew pricing (vs the ATM vols ) presents a favorable setup for constructing limited topside structures. Long digital risk reversals, preferably expressed in 9M expiry to allow for the post-

Arindam Sandilya <sup>AC</sup> (65) 6882-7759 arindam.x.sandilya@jpmorgan.com JPMorgan Chase Bank, N.A., Singapore Branch Ladislav Jankovic (1-212) 834-9618 ladislav.jankovic@jpmchase.com J.P. Morgan Securities LLC

election developments to play out, fit the bill. However, given more favorable forward points the hedge is better expressed in CNH. A 9M near zero-cost (2%) digital RR can be structured as a USD/CNH 6.95 digi put & 7.35 digi call (strikes 4.7% OTM and 0.7% OTM respectively, both close to 25% TV).

MXN continues to track previous election cycles and at \~50vols remains one of the priciest election vols in absolute terms. With significant headroom despite a more benign backdrop this time, MXN election vols could have upside. Spillover effects have elevated BRL pricing, but even so, BRL election vols, currently close to 28, are among the few that are visibly lagging behind prior cycles. Based on the 2016 election spot beta, BRL election volatilities should be 3 points higher. Flat curves, resulting from recent carry unwinds, make pre- and postelection calendars well-priced. Consider short 4M vs long 6M USD/BRL vol swaps calendar spread.

Current pricing (red marker) vs this cycle distribution.

Impact of the US election on the euro could manifest across multiple dimensions, including tariff risk and fluid geopolitical developments. The bottom line is that the multitude of possible outcomes warrants a higher volatility premium than what has typically be seen in the past US election cycles for EUR/USD and the regional proxies. With the French election just ahead it may be cleaner to hedge EUR exposure once that event risk is behind us. However, vol calendars, such as equal vega-weighted -4M / +6M delta-hedged tend to be largely immunized from occasional gamma during the life of the short leg, ensuring effective exposure to event risk specifically. Over the last 3-years of historical data, such constructs have maintained cumulative returns within +/-0.5vol bulk of the time thanks to 98% correlation between the long and short returns. Consider within G10 delta-hedged USD/SEK short 4M 25d call vs long 8M atm, in vega neutral notionals, leveraging the current SEK surface pricing, or within EM -4M / +8M USD/HUF vega neutral vol calendar, which has the flattest vol curve within EUR & CEE complex.

## Long-end defensive FX vol / skew RVs

Defensive RVs for Macro uncertainty. As suggested in the latest JPM View, the latest two consecutive soft CPI prints point toward a soft landing, yet a number of indicators suggest elevated market and growth risks, including the disconnect between market returns and manufacturing new orders/ inventories, narrow market breadth, rising jobless claims and unemployment rates. This prompts us to look for long-end defensive FX vol / skew RVs that have a good entry point, are supported by our models, do not have (much) decay, and yet have defensive properties that would play out in a risk off environment or tail risk scenario.

FX Vol mean reversion model. As the basis for our selection, we use our FX Vol mean reversion model (which we update on a weekly basis, pg 11 here). In that model, we track the 2-year dislocation in the spreads in the 1y ATM space, and trade the spread when the dislocation is higher than the +1.5 z-score. We also track additional filters such as vol carry, roll down of the curve, and the ‘stability’ of the dislocation via a short term z-score - to make sure the dislocation is not in the process of increasing. The more filters activated the better (see Figure 87).

RV spreads risk sensitivity. We then take the RV spreads (1y ATM vol) that are currently traded by the model, i.e those with a number higher than 0 in Figure 87, and we regress the monthly changes in the spreads versus the inverse monthly returns in the S&P 500 (see Figure 88). That is in order to establish which spreads would move in the ‘correct’ direction given an S&P 500 negative monthly return- that is, the higher the correlation and the beta, the better.

Selling MXN vol is punished in risk-off while selling JPY vs high-beta is favored. Out of those spreads, we can see that selling MXN vol would be heavily punished in a risk-off environment. On the other hand, selling JPY vol versus highbeta pairs such as KRW and AUD would be favored. However, given the large upside fluctuations we have seen in USD/ JPY in the last 2 years, we could expect some of those to reverse, thus increasing USD/JPY vol, if there is a global recession and the US-JP differential narrows (see JPY section in KCV ).

Buy HUF vol versus EUR vol. Given these constraints, we are left with fewer options. One that stands out is buying HUF vol versus EUR vol. It has the second highest correlation to the S&P 500 negative returns of the spreads considered, and the 4<sup>th</sup> highest beta. Thus providing a clearly defensive stance. The spread buying vol in HUF vs EUR was a great trade in the last major defensive episode during the inflation / Ukraine war crisis of 2022. Further, the term structure is in favor of the trade, thus reducing the decay, and the short term filter is also in favor. Finally, the vol carry for both vols is equivalent at around 1.2 vol pts. Further, buying 1y HUF vols is supported by our Relative Value 1Y Vega EM trade model, see here. Finally, the mean reverting properties of EUR-HUF RV spread are confirmed under a Augmented Dickey–Fuller test.

French elections. For those concerned with the French political risk premium affecting EUR vols (see latest FXMW Derivatives: French elections give FX vols a smile), the fact that both vols are part of the European bloc would provide a hedge. Hence consider:

\- Buy 1y ATM vol Swap in USD/HUF and sell in EUR/ USD for 4.4 vol pts, costs included.

RV spreads risk sensitivity to large market events. For those looking for protection from a sharp market risk-off event, we look at the regression of the same spreads but conditioning the regression on a 10%+ monthly S&P 500 drawdown (see Figure 89Beta ndcorli hS&P50 vsu -g 1%my w ,2 f ). We can see that selling JPY vols versus high-beta vols also ranks very well- but we discard the trades for the reasons discussed above (regarding the recent volatility of the JPY).

Buy AUD vol vs SEK. Another thing to note is that buying long-end AUD vols has been a good trade in large risk-off events, as a results of the strong uptick in implied AUD vols in both the GFC and also in the 2020 Covid crisis. Further, AUD vols are currently supported from a beta-to-vol perspective (see Figure 90). Indeed buying AUD vol vs JPY and SEK both show up as the top defensive RV trades given a large S&P 500 drawdown. We prefer the AUD vs SEK expression

Hence consider:

against the AUD vs JPY format given the JPY uncertainty mentioned, but also also given that the AUD-SEK spread is one of the most mean reverting spreads in the entire universe, with a half life of just 33 business days. A related trade RV but using risk reversals delta-hedged was highlighted in the latest FXMW Derivatives.

Defensive long-end skew RVs. We first look at the way we have traditionally analyzed skew richness via a two-factor regression model that regresses market quoted risk reversal vol prices (1Y 25D in this case) vs 1Y atm vols and 1Y carry, the model was introduced here. In Figure 91 we plot the current standard residual of the model vs market in the x-axis.

We can clearly see that the EUR/USD long-end risk reversal is looking expensive (more than 2 standard deviations away from fair value), this is likely a result of the French election risk premium embedded in it. On the other side of the spectrum USD/JPY risk reversal is looking cheap (0.8 std deviations away from fair value) and AUD, NZD and CHF also look slightly cheap versus fair value.

Fade French-election rich EUR risk reversal with a defensive tilt. Looking at the y-axis of the plot, we can see the correlation of short delta-hedged skew position in the currency in question versus the S&P 500 returns. We can see that shorting skew in JPY, AUD and NZD is more correlated to risk than that of CHF, SEK or EUR. This opens up the possibility of harvesting the richness in EUR skew while paring it up with a defensive long skew position in AUD or NZD - we exclude JPY given the idiosyncrasies of the Yen trade over the last couple of years. This trade would be positively exposed to overall risk, however, it would be negatively exposed to French election moves. For that reason, another option is to pair it with a Euro-bloc currency with a similar risk profile such as NOK. Hence consider:

\- Buy 1Y 25delta risk-reversal on NZD/USD (buy USD call, sell USD put) at 1.1/1.3 vols, sell 1Y 25delta risk-reversal on EUR/USD (sell USD call, buy USD put) at 1.35/1.50 vols. Same Vega on all four calls/puts legs. Keep both riskies delta-hedged.

## What to do with JPY and cross-JPY vols / vol surface

What’s going on in the JPY vol complex. JPY has kept on falling steadily in H1 24, with the end of negative rates and the (alleged) interventions in the FX market so having had little and only temporary effect in containing further Yen drops. Also, the washout of some crowded EMFX carry trade positions, at times funded via JPY shorts, did not trigger a wider repatriation of short-JPY trades by Japanese-based players, as it had occurred during previous EM Carry drawdowns.

Looking forward, possible policy normalization (rate hikes) in Japan at a time when other major central banks have started cutting rates, paired with further FX intervention risk (current level of USD/JPY is 160.9) might stimulate the interest of looking at the vol market for taking views on the currency.

Favorable entry point on the vol-carry. A high-level focus on JPY correlations (average of 3M JPY/ccy1 vs. JPY/ccy2 in the G10 space) points to the general lack of a hefty vol premium for owning JPY x-vols at present (Figure 92Decnt rypoi fwgJPY vladx-s); this observation contrasts with the EUR-x vols complex, where French election premium has triggered a significant move in the implied vols, so far not accompanied by a move in realized. Also, as reviewed in the previous section, US election JPY vol still screens favorably relative to previous cycles.

Figure 92: Decent entry point for owning JPY vol and x-vols
![](images/323a52c79f64d614bbcf3347555dba6273186c65313eea7c9fbbfcec23377d89.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan.

JPY vol surfaces setup. By looking more broadly at the current vol-surface setup in the Yen complex, vol curves are typically upward sloping up until the 6M maturity (around US elections) , then sharply downward sloping from 1y to 5Y there is no front-end premium on the curves as for EURcrosses. Pricing of vol-convexity and skew, which unlike for the EUR-crosses remains rather subdued, points to some complacency in the JPY-x complex. As a reference, the skewto-vol ratio on USD/JPY is near the top (i.e., least negative

for JPY calls) 90% quantile of its historical distribution.

Figure 93: USD/JPY too tight relative to vol and rates differentials
![](images/b7effb6626c8e221e2330c11e532b0d587bc79d599e273667018f59c4b9423d2.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan.

Skew pricing is very tight. A time series model for skew pricing based on vol and carry (see here) has found the USD/ JPY cheap since the start of 2022. While short-skew strategies across JPY/G10 did well as of late, the aforementioned arguments about the reduced appeal of selling vol ahead of JPY policy normalization and FX intervention risks apply to the skew premium as well.

Recent performance of JPY-x vol trades.Figure 94 summarizes the performance (by their risk-adjusted ratios) of different vol trades on USD/JPY since Jan 2023. Selling vol has delivered on all JPY/G10 crosses, with short-skew working in all of them beyond USD/JPY and CHF/JPY.

Vol surface trades. For USD/JPY, a long 10d vs. short 25d RV of puts is currently supported given the compressed pricing of skew and wings. However, PnL analysis since 2023 is not necessarily supportive of the RV in a pure vol format. If ever, a long 10d vs. 25d in +0.3:-1 Vegas reduces the drawdown of the short vol leg and could potentially benefit from a large downside move in spot, but that would require a multisigma move leading to the outperformance of the flies (which happens rarely). The setup looks more favorable for CHF/JPY instead (Figure 95A long10deta vs.25 RVfCHF/JPY purc hw  ), in +0.5/-1.0 Vegas.

Figure 95: A long 10delta vs. 25delta RV of CHF/JPY put reduces the drawdown for a short vol trade
![](images/9edcd3065bc1a63f55d510afc004f3138835849f2ab7a08a4a53db0677269172.jpg)
Source: J.P. Morgan.

For USD/JPY, an alternative is to consider the entry point as favorable for playing directional (i.e., non-delta-hedged) trades. The argument could be that, so far since the start of this \~25% drop in the Yen, rebounds in the Yen have been relatively limited, which has capped a possible repricing of vols. However, significant moves could get magnified by price action itself, especially if Gamma positioning by option’s market markers were to flip to negative below a certain spot level. As reviewed above, pricing of skew and convexity favor inverse ratio put spread constructs. Consider:

\- On USD/JPY, buy 6M 150 put, sell 155 put in +1.5/-1 option notionals , by receiving USD 40 bps. indic. (spot ref. 160.92)

JPY-x vol spreads. Other alternative is to consider JPY vol spreads as a way to gain positive exposure to a possible vol repricing while containing time-decay. A 2d value metric we monitor (Figure 96) compares the current vol carry for a spread (y-axis) with the beta-to-vol ratios (x-axis). At present, viable candidates across JPY-x vols could be: NOK/JPY vol swap vs. SEK/JPY vol swap, EUR/JPY straddle vs. CHF/JPY strangle. Impact of trading costs, however, limits the potential of some of these trades.

The PnL of a 3M long USD/JPY straddle vs. short 25d CHF/ JPY strangle (Figure 97USD/JPY vs.CHF olpread) meets all the required features: a) it delivers a long-term positive PnL; b) it spiked during volatility episodes; c) it is currently associated with a positive vol carry. Under the period under exam, the late-2023 rally in the

Yen is the only occurrence that could be remindful of a volatility event, although these episodes had been more ferquent and violent in the past. The spread is made more attractive given the possibility of paying vol costs on just one of tegh two legs. Consider:

\- Buy 6M USD/JPY straddle @ 9.75/10.05 indic., sell CHF/ JPY 25delta 25strangle at 9.05 ch., keep both legs deltahedged

Leveraged plays for a further drop in JPY. The correlation space offers a leveraged angle to play a further drop in the JPY. Elevated NOK/JPY via USD implied correlation (Figure 98) allows breaking a long NOK/JPY trade into the two NOK/USD higher and USD/JPY higher trades and gaining significant correlation benefits. A rise in NOK is aligned with the medium term view by the FX strategy team as contained in the latest KCV note of mid-June, while a further drop in the JPY is seen as a non-baseline scenario. For this reason, we cheapen the cost of the structure by going deep OTM on the

JPY leg (around 17 delta) while considering some buffer (2% ITMS) on the NOK leg. Consider:

\- Buy a dual-digi 6M USD/JPY >4% OTMS, USD/NOK <2% ITMS @ USD 5.2/7.2% indic. (spot ref. 160.6, 10.65, offering a \~67% discount vs. the cost of the USD/JPY digital)

## FX option trades for two “what” if scenarios

What if the DXY weakens substantially? This is not an unreasonable question given the long memory of large DXY declines in 4Q22 and ‘23, and because a mature dollar uptrend is intersecting with slowing US dataflow and OIS pricing that is well-adjusted to a consensus high-for-long Fed narrative, against the backdrop of firming global growth that has historically proven corrosive for the greenback. Two low premium suggestions for exposure to DXY weakness are as follows:

6M 10% price USD put digitals in select high carry EM FX (TRY, PHP, MXN): EM high yielders that did not participate in H1’s stretch for carry (TRY, PHP) or have recently washed out (MXN) screen as the highest bang-for-buck USD de-leveraging trades, when comparing the spot-strike distance of USD puts across currencies relative to the available chart headroom for spot to head back to their strongest YTD levels (Figure 99). The discretionary overlay on the option trade screener is that each of TRY, PHP and MXN are bottom-up long / OW recommendations by our EM strategy colleagues.

Conditional USD put switches for zero-cost lower DXY exposure (PHP vs. CNH, HUF vs. EUR): Marrying the top vs. bottom ranked entries in Figure 99 can also offer conditional exposure to USD weakness in zero-cost format (where the view expressed is that currency A will outperform currency B if the dollar weakens, but there is no exposure to USD strength since both USD put legs expire worthless in that scenario). Both CNH/PHP and EUR/ HUF satisfy our macro analysts’ pre-existing directional biases, and 3m-6m 1% - 4% USD put spread switches can be purchased in each case for zero or even negative premium (i.e. net premium intake at inception).

Screen for 6M 10% price (i.e. 10:1 geared) at-expiry digital USD puts / CCY calls based on the distance from spot-option strike as a fraction of chart headroom for USD to weaken to YTD lows (lower ratio = more attractive to buy USD put). All option pricing as of EOD 27-Jun-24. Assumes 2% charge for digital options from model mids

What if equities crack? This is likely to be a significant pain point for markets, since a near straight-line rally in SPX over the past several quarters has seen many bearish investors throw in the towel, and the acute recession anxiety of last year has given way to benign neglect. Yet the extreme concentration of the US equity rally is by now well documented, sentiment measures are almost uniformly bullish, while US unemployment rate is rising and is not far from entering the non-linear phase of the Philips curve, corporate bankruptcies

Lorenzo Ravagli, PhD (44-20) 7742-7947

are running at nearly 1Q23’s pace, and credit card delinquencies are already high in lower income segments of the population. None of these scream recession — indeed, our economic data based recession probability model is stuck at a relatively benign \~40% — but that is also precisely why portfolios are not defensively positioned and could suffer from a sharp equity drawdown. Because this is not a high odds scenario, premium commitment needs to be low, a couple of low premium suggestions are as follows:

Inverse ratio put spreads in high-beta FX (AUD/CHF, GBP/JPY): The three traditional flight-to-safety candidates in FX are USD, JPY and CHF. Of the three, we are least convicted in the USD reprising its traditionally intense LHS of the smile safe haven role — at least to the same degree as before — given pre-existing dollar length and its use as an asset rather than a funding currency over the past three years. In contrast, de-leveraging is likely to be the most intense in CHF and JPY which boast of a large short positioning base; CHF could conceivably outperform the JPY in risk-off as it did during the recent EM FX carry trade washout given the smaller exit window and absence of sticky NISA-like domestic outflows.

AUD/CHF and GBP/JPY are targets that fall out of a 10D/25D put vol ratio screener (Figure 100GBP/JY andAUDCHF screwl-pi vto u hgk q); their choice is also informed by substantial directional/macro length in AUD and GBP that will likely be flushed in a proper riskoff.

[Equities lower, FX lower] dual digitals (SX5E vs. EUR/CHF): This class of hybrids could be in business if the risk backdrop morphs into one with classical recession flavor — stocks lower alongside lower yields and stronger reserve currencies. Given ongoing political stresses in the continent, European assets appear more likely to sink in sync than their US counterparts. Consider: 6M [SX5E < 5% OTMS, EUR/CHF < 2.5% OTMS] costs 12% EUR.

## Technical Strategy

## We are looking for US dollar strength to build against EUR and GBP in the second half and for the maturing USD/JPY rally to reverse

● EUR/USD has been trading in a broad range since early 2023 with the 2024 trading range sitting in the middle of that pattern and coiling even tighter. We suspect key pattern support near 1.04 will give way in 2H24 and set the pair up for a deeper retracement of the rally from the 0.9537 Sep 2022 low. We continue to view the 1.01-1.02 area as a likely target zone for that weakness. On a near-term basis, EUR/USD triggered a cluster of our momentum divergence buy signals near 1.06-handle support levels. Those signals increase the probability for countertrend price action. Initial short-term resistance rests at 1.0763-1.0793. We continue to see the 1.09-1.10 area as a likely ceiling through the summer.

As long as GBP/USD continues to reject 2024 resistance at 1.2892-1.2915, we suspect the range eventually gives way to a bear trend in the second half. Initial medium-term trend-line support rests at 1.2341. Key medium-term support rests at the 1.2075 Sep 2022 38.2% retrace and 1.2037 4Q23 trough. Look for the pair to trend toward that area in the months ahead.

● USD/JPY trades in a late-cycle technical setup as it triggers low-frequency momentum divergence sell signals that in the past have led to trend reversals and multi-month/quarter impulses in the other direction. However, the continuation of higher highs and higher lows keeps the trend intact for now, with the market now probing the 160.33 Apr 1990 cycle peak and 160.758 Jan-Dec 2023 channel. Next resistance rests at the 163.023 Dec 2023 channel and then 164.97 Jan-Dec 2023 equal swings objective. A break through 156.188-156.721 trend support is required to suggest late-cycle is switching to end-ofcycle, in our view.

AUD/USD keeps an ambiguous short-term price pattern as it holds above the 0.6556-0.6593 cluster of moving averages, but fails to make upward progress after a number of attempts to clear the 2024 range highs and Jan 2023-Dec 2023 trend line. While that clouds the near-term outlook, we continue to think the broader cross-market setup points to an eventual slide toward longer-term range support near 0.62 in the second half.

## We expect a EUR/USD break below 1.04-handle range support in 2H24

EUR/USD tries to find some footing in the lower end of the 2024 trading range, which unfolds within an even broader holding pattern that has developed since early 2023 and on the heels of the rebound from the 0.9537 Sep 2022 trough (Figure 101EUR/SD hasbentrdig oc ly203 w4  m fp v .W uk  1  H     957   -  z ). That rally faded after meeting the 1.1275 Jan 2021 61.8% retrace, and all the subsequent price action has unfolded above the 1.0407 Sep 2022 50% retrace. From a pure-pattern perspective and despite the sharp contrast between the highly bifurcated end results for each interpretation, the 2023- 2024 price action can be viewed as bearish distribution and bullish consolidation with equal validity, in our view. The duration of DM yield curve inversion and empirical evidence that has historically indicated late-cycle environments make us lean toward the idea that the 2023-2024 price action more likely marks distribution. As such, we expect the pair to bearishly pressure broader range support in the 1.04-handles and then break down for a deeper retracement of the 2022-2023 rally leg. We suspect that weakness will not fully retrace the

rally from the 0.9537 Sep 2022 trough and instead bullish rebase near 1.01-1.02 targets that include the 4Q22 pattern objective and Sep 2022 61.8% retrace. That medium-term outlook stays firmly in gear as long as the pair is trading below the 1.09-1.10 resistance zone. Beyond that zone that has capped the market in 2024, other key medium-term resistance remains at the 1.1275 Fibonacci retracement, as noted above.

We continue to view the 1.01-1.02 area as a likely target zone for that weakness...

If it does, we would expect a stronger rebound to fade near the 1.0935 Dec 61.8% retrace again, and other nearby resistance levels as indicated on the daily chart. On the downside, continued weakness would turn our attention to other nearby levels that include the 1.063 Jul-Dec 2023 .618 swing objective, 1.0601 Apr low, 1.0597 Oct 78.6% retrace, and 1.0584 Dec-Jun .618 swing objective.

## We are looking for cable to bearishly trend toward support near 1.20 in the second half

GBP/USD stays under pressure in the near term after staging another rejection of the 1.2892 Mar high and 1.2915 Jul 2023 78.6% retrace (Figure 103). The pair also triggered momentum divergence sell signals as the pattern formed near resistance. Tactically, we think the downside pressure will build as the market presses through the nearby 50-day, 100-day, and 200-day moving averages. Bigger picture, the 2023-2024 price action looks like a bearish reversal pattern that has developed below the 1.3101 Sep 2022-Mar 2023 .618 swing objective. Key trend-line support for that pattern sits at 1.2341. We are looking for downside momentum to build in the months ahead and for the market to eventually break that support and decline toward the 1.2075 Sep 2022 38.2% retrace and 1.2037 4Q23 trough.

## USD/JPY looks like a mature bull cycle, but one that remains intact for now; watch for a trend reversal in the second half

The USD/JPY bull trend persists, but we continue to think the pair is in the terminal phase of the cycle that started with the Mar 2020 trough. The low-frequency momentum divergence sell signals present on the monthly time frame since Dec 2023 bolster that case (Figure 104, see red arrows). Historically, those bearish signals did a good job marking the terminal phases for past cycles and eventually led to multi-month/quarter period bear trends.

Figure 104: USD/JPY trades in a late-cycle technical setup as it triggers low-frequency momentum divergence sell signals that in the past have led to trend reversals and multi-month/quarter impulses in the other direction...
USD/JPY, monthly bars

The repeated rebounds from Dec 2023 channel support and the 50-day moving average in spring keep the bull trend intact for now (Figure 105). Those levels are 156.188-156.721 now, and are critical for the short- to medium-term outlook, in our view. We believe a break below that support will likely mark the first sign of a transition from late-cycle to end-of-cycle. The market is currently testing the 160.33 Apr 1990 cycle peak and 160.758 Jan-Dec 2023 channel. Next resistance rests at the 163.023 Dec 2023 channel and then 164.97 Jan-Dec 2023 equal swings objective. There is no sign of a short-term bearish reversal setup at the moment, but we are closely watching for that in the weeks ahead. On the downside, a break below the key trend support levels noted above would initially set the market up for a test of the 151.945 Oct 2022 and Oct 2023 twin peaks. The 200-day moving average is rising toward that zone now. A break through that support would confirm the longer-term change in trend and put the pair on course for a slide to the 149.25 Dec trough.

## AUD/USD keeps an ambiguous near-term price pattern within the broader range; look for the pair to test longer-term support in the second half

On a near-term basis, AUD/USD continues to hold above the 0.6556-0.6593 cluster of moving averages and thus marginally keeps a bullish bias just based on pattern analysis alone (Figure 106). However, weakness in other cyclically sensitive markets like industrial metals and equity sectors that historically have correlated with global manufacturing data trends remove the cross-market support for the bullish trend that had built in recent months. Furthermore, the pair was unable to build upon the bullish Apr-Jun trend momentum even when those other markets bolstered it. Regardless of that cross-market setup, we would respect a break above the 0.6711 Jan-Dec 2023 trend line, which would point to an extension to the 0.6819-0.6937 medium-term resistance cluster. That area has contained the price action for over a year now, and we continue to view it as a ceiling. Regardless of the whether or not the bull trend can extend, the broader contextual information provided from DM yield curves and other market trends continue to point to a late-cycle environment. To that end, our bias is for an eventual weakening of this cyclically correlated pair and retest of longerterm range support near 0.62. We suspect that can take place in the second half of the year.

While that clouds the near-term outlook, we continue to think the broader cross-market setup points to an eventual slide toward longer-term range support near 0.62 in the second half.

## Explanation of Equity Research Ratings, Designations and Analyst(s) Coverage Universe:

J.P. Morgan uses the following rating system: Overweight (over the duration of the price target indicated in this report, we expect this stock wil outperform the average total return of the stocks in the Research Analyst’s, or the Research Analyst’s team’s, coverage universe); Neutral (over the duration of the price target indicated in this report, we expect this stock will perform in line with the average total return of the stocks in the Research Analyst’s, or the Research Analyst’s team’s, coverage universe); and Underweight (over the duration of the price target indicated in this report, we expect this stock will underperform the average total return of the stocks in the Research Analyst’s, or the Research Analyst’s team’s, coverage universe. NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating and, if applicable, the price target, for this stock because of either a lack of a sufficient fundamental basis or for legal, regulatory or policy reasons. The previous rating and, if applicable, the price target, no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. In our Asia (ex-Australia and ex-India) and U.K. small- and mid-cap Equity Research, each stock’s expected total return is compared to the expected total return of a benchmark country market index, not to those Research Analysts’ coverage universe. If it does not appear in the Important Disclosures section of this report, the certifying Research Analyst’s coverage universe can be found on J.P. Morgan’s Research website, https://www.jpmorganmarkets.com

## Explanation of Emerging Markets Sovereign Research Ratings System and Valuation & Methodology:

Ratings System: J.P. Morgan uses the following issuer portfolio weightings for Emerging Markets Sovereign Research: Overweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to outperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns); Marketweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to perform in line with the relevant index, sector, or

benchmark credit returns); and Underweight (over the next three months, the recommended risk position is expected to underperform the relevant index, sector, or benchmark credit returns). NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating for this security because of either legal, regulatory or policy reasons or because of lack of a sufficient fundamental basis. The previous rating no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation is not a recommendation or a rating. NC is Not Covered. An NC designation is not a rating or a recommendation. Recommendations will be at the issuer level, and an issuer recommendation applies to all of the index-eligible bonds at the same level for the issuer. When we change the issuer-level rating, we are changing the rating for all of the issues covered, unless otherwise specified. Ratings for quasi-sovereign issuers in the EMBIG may differ from the ratings provided in EM corporate coverage.

Valuation & Methodology: For J.P. Morgan's Emerging Markets Sovereign Research, we assign a rating to each sovereign issuer (Overweight, Marketweight or Underweight) based on our view of whether the combination of the issuer’s fundamentals, market technicals, and the relative value of its securities will cause it to outperform, perform in line with, or underperform the credit returns of the EMBIGD index over the next three months. Our view of an issuer’s fundamentals includes our opinion of whether the issuer is becoming more or less able to service its deb obligations when they become due and payable, as well as whether its willingness to service debt obligations is increasing or decreasing.
